  • Reasons must be stated for any adverse or qualified statement; if unable to express an opinion on any item, say so with reasons.
  • Exception report to RBI: where a statement is unfavourable/qualified or auditor believes non-compliance with Chapter IIIB of RBI Act, Public Deposit Directions 2016, or SBR Directions 2023 → report to the concerned Regional Office of the Department of Non-Banking Supervision of RBI (First Schedule of Public Deposit Directions), where the registered office is. Report only contraventions — no statement on compliance.
  • Statutory Auditor Certificate (SAC): every NBFC submits auditor's certificate that it is engaged in NBFI business requiring CoR and is eligible to hold it, with reference to position at end of financial year (March), to the Regional Office of DNBS — format in Master Direction – NBFC Returns (Reserve Bank) Directions 2016. SM text: filed within one month of finalisation of the balance sheet and in any case before 30 December of that year.
  • CARO 2020: 3(iii) investments, guarantees, security, loans — terms not prejudicial; repayment schedule and regularity; amount overdue more than 90 days and steps taken; loans repayable on demand or without terms — aggregate, % of total loans, to promoters/related parties (s.2(76)). 3(xvi) (a) registration under s.45-IA required and obtained; (b) NBFI or housing finance activities without valid CoR; (c) CIC — continues to fulfil criteria (also exempt/unregistered CIC); (d) number of CICs in the group.

Examiner's twist (TYK Abhimanyu Finance): long story about loan growth since 2015, NOF ₹1.5 crore, applied for CoR only at year end; ask is "CARO reporting". Score: 3(xvi) — registration required? obtained? if not, reasons; NOF below required; NBFI business without CoR. Do not drift into Board-report items (that is a different report).

29 Ind AS and Division III

  • NBFCs follow Ind AS per Rule 4(1)(iv) Companies (Ind AS) Rules 2015 (amended 2023). Division III of Schedule III introduced by MCA notification 11 October 2018 for Ind AS NBFCs.
  • Division III vs Division II: (a) BS items may be presented in order of liquidity; (b) separate note for any item of "other income"/"other expenses" exceeding 1% of total income (Division II: 1% of revenue from operations or ₹10 lakh, whichever higher); (c) receivables from LLPs in which director is partner/member disclosed separately; (d) revenue from operations and OCI items on the face of the statement of P&L, not only in notes; (e) trade receivables with significant increase in credit risk and credit-impaired separately; (f) restrictions on distribution of statutory reserves in notes.
  • TYK 3: total income ₹10,000 crore → threshold ₹100 crore; labour outsourcing ₹99.50 crore → no separate disclosure needed.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Quality ControlSM Ch 1

01Ch 01 — Quality Control

Asked: Sep 2025 Q1(a) EQCR must be suitably experienced and must document the review (5 m) · Jan 2026 Q1(c) leadership responsibilities, three considerations (4 m) · May 2026 MCQ CS-I Q1 EQCR objective evaluation (2 m) · Nov 2024 Q1(a) monitoring factors (5 m) · MTP Nov 2026 EQCR and dating of report (5 m)

Two standards, two levels

  • SQC 1 = quality at the level of the firm; applies to all engagements (audits, reviews of historical financial information, other assurance and related services) and to all firms irrespective of constitution. Responsibility: CEO / managing partners.
  • SA 220 = quality at the level of the individual audit engagement; audits only. Responsibility: engagement partner. SA 220 is premised on the firm being subject to SQC 1 (SQC 1 is a sine qua non); engagement teams are entitled to rely on the firm's system unless information from the firm or others suggests otherwise.
  • Other facilitators: other SAs, Code of Ethics, certain provisions of the Companies Act, 2013. Review mechanisms: Peer Review Board, Quality Review Board, NFRA.
  • SQC 1 objective: reasonable assurance that (i) firm and personnel comply with professional standards and regulatory and legal requirements and (ii) reports issued by the firm or engagement partners are appropriate in the circumstances.

This SM covers SQC 1 and SA 220 only. SQM 1 components are not in the SM: answer every "elements" question with the six SQC 1 elements below.

SQC 1 — six elements Asked: Jan 2026 Q1(c)

  1. Leadership responsibilities for quality within the firm
  2. Ethical requirements
  3. Acceptance and continuance of client relationships and specific engagements
  4. Human resources
  5. Engagement performance
  6. Monitoring
  • Policies and procedures are documented and communicated to personnel; the firm encourages personnel to communicate their views or concerns on quality control matters (feedback).

2.1.1 Leadership responsibilities for quality Asked: Jan 2026 Q1(c)

  • Internal culture that quality is essential. CEO or managing partners assume ultimate responsibility for the system of quality control.
  • Persons given operational responsibility by the CEO/managing partners: sufficient and appropriate experience, ability, and the necessary authority.
  • Business strategy is subject to the overriding requirement to achieve quality in all engagements. Ensure: (a) management responsibilities assigned so that commercial considerations do not override quality; (b) policies on performance evaluation, compensation and promotion (including incentive systems) demonstrate the overriding commitment to quality; (c) sufficient resources for development, documentation and support of QC policies.

Examiner's twist: The story is about an expanding firm (partner good at bringing clients but weak on quality oversight; incentives on revenue; resources spent on marketing) and asks "which element?". Answer Leadership responsibilities, not Human resources, even though incentives and promotions appear: the SM places incentive systems under consideration (b) of leadership. The scoring line: "the firm's business strategy is subject to the overriding requirement to achieve quality in all engagements", then (a), (b), (c). TYK 1 (managing partner pushing a client who offers 25% higher fee, regulatory inquiries, auditor resigned) = same element plus integrity of client.

2.1.2 Ethical requirements and independence

  • Fundamental principles: integrity, objectivity, professional competence and due care, confidentiality, professional behaviour. Emphasised by: actions of leadership, awareness and training, monitoring, a process for dealing with non-compliance.
  • Independence policies (firm, personnel, experts contracted by the firm, network firm personnel) enable the firm to: (a) communicate independence requirements to personnel; (b) identify and evaluate threats and eliminate or reduce them by safeguards, or withdraw.
  • Engagement partners give the firm client information; personnel promptly notify threats; all breaches promptly notified.
  • At least annually: written confirmation of compliance with independence policies from all personnel required to be independent.
  • Familiarity threat: criteria consider (a) nature of engagement, including public interest and (b) length of service of senior personnel. Safeguards: rotation of senior personnel or EQCR. Listed entity audits: engagement partner rotated after a pre-defined period, normally not more than seven years, except where the listed audit is conducted by a sole practitioner (mandatory peer review of such firms instead).

Examiner's twist: TYK 2 — a paid CA on a bank stock audit promises a clean stock report in return for the statutory audit. Asked "how does it reflect on the QC system?": QC policies must be documented and communicated to personnel; personnel are not sensitised to SQC 1. Do not stop at "violates the Code".

2.1.3 Acceptance and continuance

  • Information decides three things: integrity of client, promoters, KMP; competence (capabilities, time, resources); compliance with ethical requirements.
  • Obtain information at three points: new client; continuing an existing engagement; new engagement with an existing client. Issues identified but client accepted → document how the issues were resolved.
  • Integrity matters (7): identity and business reputation of principal owners, key management, related parties, TCWG · nature of operations and business practices · attitude to aggressive interpretation of accounting standards and internal control environment · client aggressively concerned with keeping fees as low as possible · indications of inappropriate scope limitation · indications of money laundering or other criminal activities · reasons for proposed appointment and non-reappointment of previous firm. Knowledge grows with an ongoing relationship.
  • Capability matters (6): industry/subject knowledge · experience of regulatory or reporting requirements (or ability to gain it) · sufficient personnel · experts available · individuals eligible for EQCR available · ability to meet the reporting deadline.
  • Conflict of interest resolved before accepting.
  • Information that would have caused the firm to decline, obtained later → consider (a) professional and legal responsibilities, including reporting to the appointing person(s) or regulators; (b) possibility of withdrawing from the engagement or from both engagement and client relationship.
  • Withdrawal policies (4): discuss with appropriate level of management and TCWG the appropriate action · if withdrawing, discuss withdrawal and reasons · consider professional, regulatory or legal requirement to remain in place, or to report the withdrawal and reasons to regulators · document significant issues, consultations, conclusions and basis.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Quality ControlSM Ch 1

2.1.4 Human resources

  • Sufficient personnel with capabilities, competence and commitment to ethical principles. Covers recruitment, compensation, training, career development, performance evaluation; emphasis on CPD.
  • Each engagement assigned to an engagement partner. Policies require: (a) identity and role of EP communicated to key members of client management and TCWG; (b) EP has appropriate capabilities, competence, authority and time; (c) EP's responsibilities clearly defined and communicated to the EP.

2.1.5 Engagement performance — consultation, EQCR, differences Asked: Sep 2025 Q1(a); May 2026 MCQ

  • Consistency through briefing teams, processes for complying with standards, supervision and training, methods of review, documentation.
  • Consultation on difficult or contentious matters, with people within or outside the firm with specialised expertise; a firm without internal resources may use advisory services of other firms or professional and regulatory bodies; document issues and results.
  • EQCR: significant judgments reviewed for an objective view before the report is issued. Extent depends on complexity and risk of an inappropriate report. Does not reduce the engagement partner's responsibility. Mandatory for all audits of financial statements of listed entities; for others the firm devises criteria.
  • EQCR for listed audits (SQC 1, 8 points): team's evaluation of firm's independence · significant risks and responses · judgments, particularly materiality and significant risks · appropriate consultation on differences of opinion / contentious matters and conclusions · significance and disposition of corrected and uncorrected misstatements · matters to be communicated to management, TCWG and, where applicable, regulators · whether working papers selected reflect work on significant judgments and support conclusions · appropriateness of the report to be issued.
  • Who can be EQCR: a partner, other person in the firm (member of ICAI), suitably qualified external person (e.g. partner or experienced employee of another firm), or a team of such individuals. For listed audits: sufficient and appropriate experience and authority to act as audit engagement partner on listed entity audits.
  • Objectivity: EQCR must not participate in the engagement or make decisions for the team. EP may consult the EQCR during the engagement without compromising eligibility; if consultations become significant and objectivity cannot be kept, another individual within the firm or a suitably qualified external person takes the role of EQCR or of the person consulted. Firm policies provide for replacement of an EQCR whose objectivity may be impaired.
  • Differences of opinion (within team, with those consulted, EP vs EQCR): report issued only after resolution. EP rejects EQCR's recommendation → firm's established procedures, e.g. consult another practitioner or firm, or a professional or regulatory body.

Examiner's twist: (1) TYK 4 — team got an ICAI expert opinion, so "EQCR lost relevance": wrong; the expert opinion addresses interpretation, EQCR is a separate mandatory requirement for listed audits. (2) Sep 2025 — a person with no listed-audit experience "signs a few working papers": fails both eligibility (experience and authority to act as EP on listed audits) and the documentation requirement. (3) EQCR "decides the discount rate" for the team: objectivity lost, replace.

Engagement documentation — assembly, ownership, retention

  • Assembly: complete final engagement files on a timely basis after reports are finalised — not more than 60 days after the date of the auditor's report for audits; other engagements: limits appropriate to the engagement. Two or more reports on the same subject matter (e.g. component report for group consolidation, later statutory report) → time limit applied to each report as a separate engagement.
  • Policies maintain confidentiality, safe custody, integrity, accessibility and retrievability.
  • EQCR documentation (SQC 1): (a) firm's EQCR procedures performed; (b) EQCR completed before the report is issued; (c) reviewer not aware of unresolved matters making significant judgments/conclusions inappropriate.
  • Ownership: documentation is the property of the firm unless law or regulation says otherwise; extracts may be given to clients if this does not undermine validity of the work or independence.
  • Retention: long enough for monitoring, or longer if law requires. Audits: ordinarily no shorter than seven years from the date of the auditor's report, or, if later, the date of the group auditor's report.
  • Scanned paper documents (TYK answer 9): copies reflect entire content including manual signatures, cross-references and annotations; integrated into files with indexing and sign-off; retrievable and printable. Working papers should show the source (client-prepared vs team-prepared).

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Quality ControlSM Ch 1

2.1.6 Monitoring Asked: Nov 2024 Q1(a)

  • Policies are relevant, adequate, operating effectively and complied with in practice; include an ongoing consideration and evaluation of the system, including a periodic inspection of a selection of completed engagements.
  • Factors (6): whether the QC system is appropriately designed and effectively implemented · whether new developments in professional standards and legal/regulatory requirements are reflected · monitoring entrusted to a partner or other person with sufficient and appropriate experience and authority · dealing with complaints and allegations of non-compliance (from within or outside the firm) · remedial action against personnel who did not conform · action when deficiencies in design/operation or non-compliance are identified.

SA 220 — objective and the six matters Asked: Sep 2025 Q1(a); MTP Nov 2026

  • Objective: QC procedures at engagement level giving reasonable assurance that (a) the audit complies with professional standards and regulatory and legal requirements; (b) the auditor's report is appropriate. TYK 5: CARO clause vii(a) reported as "generally regular" → not qualitative, audit does not comply with regulatory and legal requirements → breaches SA 220 objective.
  • 3.1 Leadership on audits: EP takes responsibility for overall quality on each audit; emphasises (a) importance of (i) complying with standards and law, (ii) complying with firm's QC policies, (iii) issuing appropriate reports, (iv) team's ability to raise concerns without fear of reprisals; (b) quality is essential.
  • 3.2 Ethical: identify a threat to independence that safeguards may not reduce; report to relevant persons within the firm for action — eliminate the activity/interest or withdraw where legally permitted. EP forms a conclusion on compliance with independence, using information from the firm and network firms.
  • 3.3 Acceptance/continuance: on SQC 1 lines plus significant matters arising in the current or previous engagement and their implications.
  • 3.4 Assignment of engagement teams: team and any auditor's experts not part of the team collectively have appropriate competence and capabilities.
  • 3.5 Engagement performance: EP responsible for direction, supervision, performance; appropriate report; review of audit documentation before the report; sufficient appropriate evidence; consultation on difficult or contentious matters.
  • 3.6 EQCR — EP shall: (a) determine that an EQCR has been appointed; (b) discuss significant matters, including those identified in the EQCR, with the reviewer; (c) not date the auditor's report until completion of the EQCR.
  • EQCR evaluation (all EQCRs): (a) discussion of significant matters with EP; (b) review of FS and proposed report; (c) review of selected documentation on significant judgments; (d) evaluation of conclusions and appropriateness of proposed report. Listed — also: (a) team's evaluation of firm's independence; (b) appropriate consultation on differences/contentious matters; (c) documentation selected reflects work on significant judgments.
  • Differences of opinion: team follows firm's policies for resolving them.
  • 3.7 Monitoring: EP considers results of the firm's monitoring as shown in the latest information circulated by the firm (and network firms), and whether deficiencies may affect the audit.
  • 3.8 Documentation — EP: (a) ethical issues and how resolved; (b) conclusions on independence and supporting discussions; (c) conclusions on acceptance/continuance; (d) nature, scope and conclusions of consultations. EQCR documents: (a) firm's EQCR procedures performed; (b) EQCR completed on or before the date of the auditor's report; (c) not aware of unresolved matters.

Examiner's twist: Fieldwork done, EP review done, report dated 15 May, EQCR completed 18 May (TYK 10; MTP Nov 2026 has 25 May vs 28 May). The story stresses "sufficient evidence already obtained". The scoring line: EP shall not date the report until completion of the EQCR; SA 700 dates the report not earlier than the date of sufficient appropriate evidence, and for listed audits the EQCR assists in determining whether that evidence has been obtained. Matters from EQCR "considered subsequently" is not allowed — they must be resolved on or before the report date.

Examiner's twist: A case about a listed company's audit quality, asking "which body reviews?" → NFRA (listed falls under Rule 3); QRB can review only entities outside Rule 3 or those referred by NFRA. A sole practitioner auditing a listed entity → no rotation, but mandatory peer review.

Beyond the SM (practical): firms usually run a pre-issuance checklist that matches the auditor's report with the FS (profit vs loss, cash flow statement exempt for small company), the very lapses in CA Jagriti's opening case.

02Ch 02 — General Auditing Principles and Auditor's Responsibilities

Asked: Sep 2025 Q2(a) SA 250 two categories, Fire NOC (5 m) · Sep 2025 MCQ SA 299 joint auditor (2 m) · Jan 2026 Q5(b) SA 240 fraud risk factors (5 m) · Jan 2026 Q6(c) OR SA 240 withdrawal (4 m) · May 2026 Q2(a) SA 260 significant findings (5 m) · May 2026 MCQs SA 250 other laws, SA 402 Type 2 reference · May 2025 Q6(a) SA 402 Type 1/2 · MTP Jan 2026 SA 299 allocation

SA 200 / 210 / 230 — what this SM gives you

  • SA 200: overall responsibilities; plan and perform with professional skepticism; misstatements arise from errors or frauds. Inherent limitations of SA 200 are greater for fraud and for non-compliance with laws (below).
  • SM note: SA 200, SA 210 and SA 230 are covered in depth at Intermediate; at Final their application may come as a case study.
  • Engagement letter links in this chapter: auditor's responsibilities are often in the engagement letter; where law does not require TCWG to agree the terms, giving TCWG a copy of the engagement letter may be an appropriate way to communicate them (SA 260). Agreed terms recorded in the engagement letter include the expected form and content of the auditor's report. Joint auditors obtain a common engagement letter and a common management representation letter (SA 299).
  • Documentation time limits used in answers come from SQC 1 (Ch 1): assembly ≤ 60 days after report date; retention ordinarily ≥ 7 years.

SA 240 — objectives, fraud, two types Asked: Jan 2026 Q5(b), Q6(c)

  • Objectives: (a) identify and assess RoMM due to fraud; (b) obtain SAAE on assessed risks through appropriate responses; (c) respond appropriately to identified or suspected fraud.
  • Fraud = intentional act by management, TCWG, employees or third parties, using deception to obtain an unjust or illegal advantage. Fraud vs error = intentional or unintentional. Auditor is concerned only with fraud causing material misstatement and does not make legal determinations.
  • Two types: fraudulent financial reporting and misappropriation of assets; both involve incentive/pressure, perceived opportunity, rationalisation.
  • FFR accomplished by: manipulation, falsification (incl. forgery) or alteration of records · misrepresentation or intentional omission of events/transactions · intentional misapplication of accounting principles. Management may want to reduce earnings (tax) or inflate them (bank financing).
  • Override techniques (6): fictitious JEs near period end · inappropriate adjustment of assumptions/judgments for estimates · omitting, advancing or delaying recognition · concealing facts · complex transactions structured to misrepresent · altering records and terms of significant unusual transactions.
  • Misappropriation (often employees, small amounts; management can conceal better): embezzling receipts · stealing physical assets or IP · paying for goods/services not received (fictitious vendors/employees, kickbacks) · personal use of assets (collateral for personal loan).

Who is responsible

  • Primary responsibility for prevention and detection: TCWG and management; emphasis on prevention (reduces opportunity) and deterrence (likelihood of detection and punishment); culture of honesty, active oversight by TCWG.
  • Auditor: reasonable assurance that FS as a whole are free from material misstatement, whether fraud or error. Risk of not detecting fraud > error (concealment, forgery, collusion — collusion makes false evidence look persuasive). Detection depends on skill of perpetrator, frequency and extent of manipulation, degree of collusion, size of amounts, seniority. Management fraud is harder to detect than employee fraud.
  • Auditor maintains skepticism, considers management override, and recognises that procedures effective for error may not detect fraud.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · SA 240SM Ch 2

Fraud risk factors Asked: Jan 2026 Q5(b)

  • Definition: events or conditions indicating an incentive or pressure to commit fraud or providing an opportunity. They do not necessarily indicate fraud, but have often been present where fraud occurred. Cannot easily be ranked; whether present and relevant is a matter of professional judgment.
HeadFraudulent financial reporting (examples)Misappropriation of assets (examples)
Incentives / pressuresFinancial stability threatened: competition with declining margins; rapid change/obsolescence/interest rates; falling demand; operating losses threatening bankruptcy or takeover; negative operating cash flows while reporting earnings growth; rapid growth or unusual profitability vs industry; new accounting/statutory requirements. Third-party pressure: analysts' expectations, optimistic press releases; need for debt/equity financing (R&D, capex); marginal ability to meet listing or covenant requirements; effect of poor results on pending business combinations or contracts. Personal finances of management/TCWG: significant financial interests; bonuses/options/earn-outs on aggressive targets; personal guarantees. Excessive pressure to meet TCWG targets.Personal financial obligations; adverse relationships with employees having access to cash/assets: known or anticipated layoffs, changes to compensation or benefit plans, promotions/rewards inconsistent with expectations.
OpportunitiesSignificant RPTs outside ordinary course or with related entities not audited / audited by another firm; dominance allowing non-arm's-length terms; significant estimates difficult to corroborate; unusual complex transactions near period end ("substance over form"); operations across borders; intermediaries without business justification; bank accounts/subsidiaries in tax havens without justification. Ineffective monitoring of management: domination by one person or small group (non owner-managed) without compensating controls; ineffective TCWG oversight. Complex/unstable structure: unclear controlling interest; unusual legal entities; high turnover of senior management, legal counsel, TCWG. Deficient internal control: inadequate monitoring of controls; high turnover or ineffective accounting/IA/IT staff; ineffective accounting systems.Large cash on hand; inventory small, high value or in high demand; easily convertible assets (bearer bonds, diamonds, computer chips); small marketable fixed assets. Inadequate controls: segregation of duties, oversight of senior management expenses, supervision of remote locations, job applicant screening, record keeping, authorisation, physical safeguards, reconciliations, documentation of returns, no mandatory vacations for key control staff, management's poor IT understanding, access controls over event logs.
Attitudes / rationalisationsIneffective communication of values/ethics; non-financial management's preoccupation with accounting policies/estimates; known history of securities law violations or fraud claims; excessive interest in stock price/earnings trend; committing to aggressive forecasts; failing to remedy known significant deficiencies; minimising earnings for tax; low morale of senior management; owner-manager mixes personal and business; shareholder disputes in closely held entity; justifying marginal accounting on materiality; strained relationship with current/predecessor auditor (disputes, unrealistic time constraints, restricted access, domineering behaviour).Disregard for monitoring; overriding controls or not remedying deficiencies; displeasure with entity; changes in behaviour or lifestyle; tolerance of petty theft.

Examiner's twist: The story lists 6–8 facts and asks only for "incentives/pressures and opportunities for FFR" (Jan 2026). Do not list attitudes; label each fact under its head. Traps: "failure to act on internal auditor's deficiencies" is an attitude (TYK 2), not an opportunity; "need for huge capex to stay competitive" is pressure (TYK 1: revenue 750 → 1,000 crore with no capacity change); "high turnover of accounting staff" is an opportunity (deficient internal control); "small high-value inventory" is misappropriation opportunity, not FFR.

Skepticism, team discussion, risk assessment

  • Skepticism throughout, notwithstanding past experience of honesty and integrity of management and TCWG. Records may be accepted as genuine unless reason to believe otherwise; doubt on authenticity or undisclosed modified terms → investigate further; inconsistent responses of management/TCWG → investigate.
  • Team discussion: how and where FS are susceptible to fraud, notwithstanding beliefs that management and TCWG are honest.
  • Risk assessment: inquiries of management and others; how TCWG oversee management's fraud-risk process and controls; unexpected relationships from analytical procedures; evaluate whether fraud risk factors are present.
  • Revenue recognition: presumption of fraud risk; may be rebutted, e.g. single type of simple revenue (leasehold revenue from a single unit rental property). Rebutted → document reasons. Complex revenue or weak controls → high probability of fraud risk (TYK 14, Arihant ₹43 crore unrecorded bookings, part in CFO's account).
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · SA 240SM Ch 2

Responses to fraud risk

  • FS level (3): (a) assign and supervise personnel according to knowledge, skill and ability and the fraud risk; (b) evaluate whether selection and application of accounting policies (subjective measurements, complex transactions) indicate earnings management; (c) incorporate unpredictability in nature, timing and extent.
  • Assertion level: change NTE for more reliable/relevant evidence. SM example: earnings pressure → risk of inflated sales (terms precluding recognition, invoicing before delivery) → external confirmations of sales agreement details (date, rights of return, delivery terms) plus inquiries of non-financial personnel.
  • Management override: present in all entities; unpredictable → a fraud risk and a significant risk. Irrespective of assessment: (a) test journal entries and other adjustments; (b) review estimates for bias; (c) evaluate business rationale of significant transactions outside normal course or unusual. Determine whether other procedures are needed.
  • Evaluation: final analytical procedures may show an unrecognised fraud risk. Misstatement indicative of fraud → consider reliability of representations; fraud is unlikely to be an isolated occurrence. Misstatement (material or not) that may be fraud involving senior management → re-evaluate fraud risk and NTE; consider collusion with employees, management or third parties when reconsidering evidence.

Unable to continue — exceptional circumstances Asked: Jan 2026 Q6(c) OR

  • (a) Determine professional and legal responsibilities, including any requirement to report to the person(s) who made the appointment or to regulators;
  • (b) Consider whether it is appropriate to withdraw, where withdrawal is legally permitted;
  • (c) If withdrawing: (i) discuss withdrawal and reasons with appropriate level of management and TCWG; (ii) determine whether there is a professional or legal requirement to report the withdrawal and reasons to the appointing person(s) or regulators.

Examiner's twist: PMP Industries (₹20 crore fictitious sales, ₹8 crore missing inventory, MD threatens legal action, Board divided). The examiner wants the three-step list, not an opinion paragraph. One line that scores: "withdrawal only where legally permitted". Also note fraud communication to TCWG and, from the SM's TYK 14 answer, reporting under section 143(12) of frauds above ₹1 crore to the Central Government within the prescribed time.

Representations, communication, documentation

  • Written representations from management (and TCWG where applicable) (4): (a) responsibility for internal control to prevent and detect fraud; (b) disclosed results of management's fraud risk assessment; (c) disclosed knowledge of fraud/suspected fraud involving management, employees with significant roles in internal control, or others where material; (d) disclosed allegations of fraud communicated by employees, former employees, analysts, regulators or others.
  • Management: fraud identified or indicated → communicate on a timely basis to the appropriate level of management.
  • TCWG (unless all TCWG manage the entity): fraud involving management, employees with significant roles in IC, or others causing material misstatement → timely. Suspected management fraud → communicate suspicions and discuss NTE of procedures needed to complete the audit. Plus any other fraud matters relevant to TCWG.
  • Outside the entity: determine whether there is a responsibility to report; legal duty may override confidentiality (e.g. bank auditor → RBI). Some entities: duty to report misstatements where management and TCWG fail to act.
  • Documentation: significant decisions from team discussion; identified and assessed fraud risks at FS and assertion level; overall responses and NTE linked to assertion-level risks; results, including management-override procedures; communications about fraud to management, TCWG, regulators and others; reasons for rebutting the revenue presumption.

SA 250 — scope, two categories, objectives Asked: Sep 2025 Q2(a); May 2026 MCQ

  • Does not apply to other assurance engagements where the auditor is specifically engaged to test and report separately on compliance with laws.
  • Heavily regulated (banks, chemical companies) vs only operating laws (occupational safety and health). Non-compliance → fines, litigation, other consequences that may be material.
  • Objectives: (a) SAAE on compliance with laws having a direct effect on material amounts and disclosures; (b) specified procedures to identify non-compliance with other laws that may be material; (c) respond appropriately to identified or suspected non-compliance.
  • Responsibility for compliance: management, with oversight of TCWG. Entity policies (7): monitor legal requirements; internal control; code of conduct; training; monitor code and discipline; legal advisors; register of significant laws and record of complaints. Larger entities: internal audit, audit committee, compliance function.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · SA 250SM Ch 2
PointCategory (a): direct-effect lawsCategory (b): other laws
NatureGenerally recognised to have a direct effect on determination of material amounts and disclosures — e.g. tax and labour laws; form and content of FS; industry-specific reportingNo direct effect on amounts/disclosures but compliance may be fundamental to operating aspects, ability to continue business, or avoiding material penalties — e.g. operating licence, regulatory solvency, environmental regulations
Auditor's responsibilityObtain sufficient appropriate audit evidence about complianceLimited to specified audit procedures: (a) inquire of management and, where appropriate, TCWG whether in compliance; (b) inspect correspondence with licensing or regulatory authorities
Trigger words"GST", "income-tax", "PF/ESI", "gratuity", "Schedule III""licence", "NOC", "pollution board", "Net Owned Funds", "certificate of registration", "factory permit"
  • Fundamental-effect laws may cause the entity to cease operations or question going concern: bank — capital or investment requirements; NBFC — failure to obtain certificate of registration under RBI Act or NOF below the RBI limit.
  • Auditor is not responsible for preventing non-compliance and cannot be expected to detect all. Inherent limitations are greater because: many operating laws do not affect FS and are not captured by information systems; non-compliance may be concealed (collusion, forgery, failure to record, override, misrepresentation); whether an act is non-compliance is ultimately for a court of law. The further removed from FS, the less likely the auditor is to notice.
  • Understanding: (a) legal and regulatory framework of entity and industry; (b) how the entity complies. Remain alert during other procedures. Written representations: all known instances of non-compliance or suspected non-compliance have been disclosed.
  • When identified/suspected: obtain (a) understanding of the nature of the act and circumstances; (b) further information on possible effect on FS. Discuss with management (and TCWG). No sufficient information and effect may be material → consider legal advice. Still insufficient → evaluate effect on opinion. Evaluate implications for risk assessment and reliability of written representations.

SA 250 — reporting

To whomRule
TCWGUnless all TCWG are in management (and already aware): communicate non-compliance coming to attention, other than clearly inconsequential. Intentional and material → as soon as practicable. Management or TCWG involved → next higher level (audit committee, supervisory board); none exists, or communication may not be acted upon, or unsure whom to report → consider legal advice.
Auditor's report — material, not adequately reflectedQualified or adverse (SA 705) — e.g. child labour (TYK answer 6), Fire NOC (Sep 2025)
Auditor's report — precluded by management/TCWGQualified or disclaimer (scope limitation, SA 705)
Auditor's report — limitation imposed by circumstancesEvaluate effect on opinion per SA 705
RegulatorsDetermine whether there is a responsibility to report outside the entity
  • Documentation: identified or suspected non-compliance and results of discussion with management, TCWG and outside parties.

Examiner's twist: Management says "compliance with child labour / Fire NOC law is outside your financial audit" (TYK 6, Sep 2025). Answer structure: the auditor is not responsible for preventing non-compliance, but for other laws he performs specified procedures; non-compliance can lead to penalties with material effect → check penal provisions and disclosure → if material and not reflected, qualified or adverse. MCQ trap (May 2026): for licence-type laws, "obtain SAAE on compliance" is wrong; the answer is "limited to specified procedures (inquiry, inspection of correspondence)". DGGI notice for fictitious invoices (TYK 3) = suspected non-compliance with a direct-effect law (GST) plus IRDAI regulations.

AAA · Twist notes · SA 260SM Ch 2

SA 260 — TCWG and objectives Asked: May 2026 Q2(a)

  • TCWG: person(s) or organisation(s) (e.g. corporate trustee) responsible for overseeing strategic direction and accountability, including the financial reporting process; may include management (executive board members, owner-manager). Governance not formally defined (family-owned, some NPOs) → discuss and agree with the engaging party; SA 315 understanding of governance is relevant; appropriate persons may vary by matter.
  • Two-way communication assists: (a) understanding audit matters in context and a constructive relationship with independence; (b) auditor obtaining information from TCWG; (c) TCWG overseeing financial reporting, reducing RoMM.
  • Objectives (4): communicate auditor's responsibilities and overview of planned scope and timing; obtain information; provide timely observations that are significant; promote effective two-way communication.

What to communicate (four heads)

  1. Auditor's responsibilities: forming and expressing an opinion on FS prepared by management with TCWG oversight; audit does not relieve management or TCWG of their responsibilities.
  2. Planned scope and timing, including significant risks. Does not change the auditor's sole responsibility for strategy and plan. Care: communicating detailed procedures may make them predictable, especially where TCWG manage the entity.
  3. Significant findings: (i) views on significant qualitative aspects of accounting practices — policies, estimates, disclosures; explain why an acceptable practice is not most appropriate; (ii) significant difficulties; (iii) unless all TCWG manage: significant matters discussed/corresponded with management, and written representations requested; (iv) circumstances affecting form and content of the report; (v) other significant matters relevant to oversight.
  4. Independence — listed entities: statement of compliance with independence requirements (team, firm, network firms); all relationships and other matters bearing on independence, including total fees for audit and non-audit services by firm and network firms to the entity and components it controls, allocated to categories; related safeguards.
Sub-listSM itemsTrigger words
Significant difficulties (6)Significant delays, unavailable personnel, unwillingness to provide information · unreasonably brief time · extensive unexpected effort · unavailability of expected information · restrictions by management · unwillingness to make or extend going-concern assessment. May become a scope limitation → modified opinion"delay", "deadline", "refused data"
Significant matters discussed with management (5)Significant events/transactions · business conditions and plans affecting RoMM · concerns about management consulting other accountants · discussions on appointment re accounting practices, auditing standards or fees · disagreements (except initial differences later resolved)"opinion shopping", "fee discussion at appointment"
Form and content of report (5)Modified opinion (SA 705) · MURGC (SA 570) · KAMs (SA 701) · EOM/OMP (SA 706) · uncorrected material misstatement of other information (SA 720). A draft report may be given to TCWG"expected to modify", "KAM", "other information"
  • Process: communicate form, timing and expected general content. In writing: significant findings where oral would not be adequate (need not include all matters); independence for listed entities. On a timely basis. Evaluate adequacy of two-way communication; if inadequate, evaluate effect on risk assessment and evidence and take action.
  • Documentation: oral → record in documentation when and to whom; written → retain a copy.
  • KAM timing (TYK 4, via SA 701): preliminary views may be communicated when discussing planned scope and timing and discussed further with findings; a draft report may help.

Examiner's twist: (1) A listed company's audit committee asks the auditor to "justify independence" and she thinks it is beyond its purview (TYK) — wrong: SA 260 requires the independence communication in listed entities, and the audit committee reviews independence under the Companies Act. (2) May 2026: story about a discount-rate estimate and management delays; the ask is "what to communicate" → qualitative aspects of estimates and significant difficulties, under "significant findings". (3) "Auditor listed responsibilities, scope and timing, independence — what is missing?" → significant findings (TYK 9).

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · SA 299SM Ch 2

SA 299 — joint audit Asked: Sep 2025 MCQ; MTP Jan 2026 S-II 3(a)

  • Joint audit = audit of FS of an entity by two or more auditors appointed with the objective of issuing the audit report. Section 139(3): members may resolve that the audit be conducted by more than one auditor. SA 299 does not deal with principal auditor vs auditor of a component.
  • Objectives (4): broad principles; uniform approach; identify distinct areas of work and coverage; identify individual and joint responsibility.
  • Planning: EP and key members of each joint auditor involved; overall audit strategy established jointly. Before commencement, a joint audit plan: (a) division of audit areas and common areas; (b) reporting objectives, timing, communications; (c) significant factors directing team efforts; (d) results of preliminary engagement activities and relevant earlier knowledge; (e) NTE of resources.
  • Division usually by identifiable units or specified areas; if not possible, by items of assets, liabilities, income or expenditure; some important areas not divided and covered by all.
  • RoMM assessed by each joint auditor, communicated to others and documented. NTE for common and allotted areas discussed, documented and communicated to TCWG.
  • Common engagement letter and common management representation letter. Work allocation document signed by all joint auditors and communicated to TCWG (avoids disputes among joint auditors and with the entity).
Individual responsibility of each joint auditorJoint and several responsibility of all
Work allocated to him, including proper execution of procedures · obtaining and evaluating information and explanations for allotted divisions/zones/units · NTE of procedures for allotted areas · study and evaluation of internal control and risk assessment for allotted areas · execution of procedures in common areas assigned to him (TYK 5: DES & Associates' batch-processing tests)(a) work not divided and carried out by all; (b) planning decisions on NTE in common areas — only the appropriateness of the decisions; (c) matters brought to notice by any one and agreed by all; (d) examining that FS comply with relevant statutes; (e) presentation and disclosure as per the framework; (f) audit report complies with statutes, SAs and ICAI pronouncements
Trigger words: "performed by X's team", "zone allotted to X" → individual; "decided jointly", "not divided", "Schedule III disclosure", "report wording" → joint and several. Obtaining information and explanations from management is shared by all unless a specific pattern is agreed.
  • A matter relevant to another joint auditor's area → communicate to all other joint auditors in writing prior to completion of the audit.
  • Reporting: common report; disagreement → separate report; not bound by majority; each report refers to the other under "Other Matter Paragraph" (SA 706). Division/branch audited by one → others may proceed on the basis that its FS comply and give a true and fair view. Discuss conclusions with each other before finalising.
  • Each may assume: (a) the others performed their part in accordance with SAs — no need to review their work or test it; (b) the others brought to notice any departure from the framework or significant observations.
  • TCWG: expected modification → circumstances and proposed wording (SA 705); expected EOM/OMP → expectation and proposed wording (SA 706).

Examiner's twist: MTP Jan 2026 / TYK 10 (Tree Ltd / Studio Ltd): understatement of receivables, valuation looked after by one firm, no documentation of division. Two points score: that firm is responsible for allotted work including execution, and SA 299 is violated because the allocation was not documented, signed and communicated to TCWG. 2-1 disagreement on an adverse opinion (TYK Q1): the minority auditor issues a separate report; reference by OMP, not EOM.

SA 402 — service organisations Asked: May 2025 Q6(a); May 2026 MCQ

  • Expands how the user auditor applies SA 315 and SA 330. Definitions: service organisation (third party or segment providing services that are part of user entities' information systems relevant to financial reporting); user entity; service auditor (reports on SO's controls at the request of the SO); user auditor.
  • Relevant when services and controls are part of the user entity's information system — they affect: (a) significant classes of transactions; (b) procedures by which transactions are initiated, recorded, processed, corrected, transferred to GL, reported; (c) related accounting records; (d) how events and conditions other than transactions are captured; (e) financial reporting process including significant estimates and disclosures; (f) controls over journal entries including non-standard ones. Controls over safeguarding of assets may also be relevant.
  • Objectives: (a) understand nature and significance of services and effect on internal control, sufficient to identify and assess RoMM; (b) design and perform responsive procedures.

Trigger words"as at 31 December", "design", "understanding""throughout the period", "operating effectively", "tests performed by service auditor" (TYK 12 Happy Hospital → Type 2)

The four look-alike procedure lists in SA 402

  • Complementary user entity controls: controls the SO assumes in its design will be implemented by user entities, identified in its system description.
  • Responding (SA 330): (a) is SAAE available from records at the user entity; if not, (b) further procedures or another auditor at the SO.
  • Fraud / NOCLAR / uncorrected misstatements at the SO: inquire of user entity management whether the SO reported, or management is aware of, any; evaluate effect on procedures, conclusions and report.
  • Reporting: unable to obtain SAAE on the services → modify under SA 705. No reference to the service auditor's work in an unmodified report unless required by law or regulation; if required, state that it does not diminish the user auditor's responsibility. Reference relevant to understanding a modification → state that it does not diminish responsibility.

Examiner's twist: The case gives a big percentage (payroll 63% or 75% of costs), a Type 2 report, even a sub-service organisation and "carve-out method", and then asks only whether the reviewer is right to delete the reference to the service auditor in an unmodified report (TYK 13, May 2026 MCQ). Answer: yes — no reference in an unmodified report unless law or regulation requires it. Do not drift into sub-service organisation methods; the SM does not develop them.

AAA · Twist notes · Audit Planning, Strategy & ExecutionSM Ch 3

03Ch 03 — Audit Planning, Strategy & Execution

Asked: Sep 2025 MCQ CS-III Q10 (SA 620 evaluating expert) · Sep 2025 Q3(b) (SA 540 significant-risk estimate) · Jan 2026 Q1(b) (SA 450 uncorrected misstatements) · May 2026 MCQ CS-I Q5 (SA 620) · May 2026 MCQ CS-II Q8 (SA 610). Also ICAI May 2025 Q2(b) SA 620, MTP Sep 2025 5(b) SA 540 prior-period review.

SA 300 — Planning: benefits, nature, continuous, responsibility

  • Benefits (6): attention to important areas; timely resolution of potential problems; proper organisation and management of the engagement; proper selection of engagement team; direction and supervision of the team; easy coordination with auditors of components and experts.
  • Nature of planning varies with: size and complexity of auditee; past experience and expertise of key team members; change in circumstances.
  • Continuous process: not a discrete phase; begins shortly after the previous audit and continues until completion of the current one. Includes: analytical procedures as risk assessment procedures, general understanding of legal and regulatory framework, determination of materiality, involvement of experts, other risk assessment procedures.
  • Responsibility: elements may be discussed with management (to coordinate procedures), but overall audit strategy and audit plan remain the auditor's responsibility. Discussing nature and timing of detailed procedures may make them too predictable. Engagement partner and other key members should be involved in planning.
  • Preliminary activities at the beginning: (i) SA 220 procedures on continuance of client relationship; (ii) evaluating compliance with ethical requirements incl. independence; (iii) understanding of terms of engagement under SA 210.
  • Contents of audit plan (3): NTE of planned risk assessment procedures (SA 315); NTE of planned further audit procedures at assertion level (SA 330); other planned procedures required by SAs. Extent includes sample sizes (TYU 2 — deciding sample size later is not proper).
  • Changes: update strategy and plan due to (i) unexpected events, (ii) changes in conditions, (iii) audit evidence obtained — e.g. substantive evidence contradicting tests of controls; also change in law, notifications, government policies.

Overall audit strategy — factors, benefits, considerations, documentation

  • Strategy sets the scope, timing and direction of the audit and guides the audit plan.
  • Documentation (3): overall audit strategy; audit plan; significant changes during the engagement and the reasons (TYU 3 — NGO/FCRA: extended procedures must be documented with reasons).
  • Relationship: strategy is prepared before the plan; plan is more detailed; change in one changes the other (CA Sam: lowered materiality → first modify strategy, then plan). Audit programme divergence: first modify the overall plan, then the programme.
ListItems (SM)Trigger words in question → which one
2.1 Factors while establishing strategy (5)Characteristics that define scope; reporting objectives (timing, communications); factors significant in directing team's efforts; results of preliminary engagement activities and knowledge from other engagements; nature, timing, extent of resources."factors to be considered in establishing / formulating overall audit strategy"
2.2 Benefits — strategy helps determine (4)Qualitative resources (experienced members for high-risk areas, experts on complex matters); quantity of resources (team at inventory count, budget hours); timing of deployment (interim / cut-off); management of resources (briefings, reviews, EQCR)."what matters does the strategy assist the auditor to determine", "allocation of resources"
2.3 Considerations — 4 heads(a) Characteristics of engagement (FRF, industry reporting, components, service organisations, IT effect, client personnel); (b) Reporting objectives, timing, communications; (c) Significant factors, preliminary activities, knowledge gained (materiality, higher-risk areas, skepticism, previous deficiencies, business developments); (d) NTE of resources (team selection, budgeting)."examples of matters to consider", "new venture / online shopping added" (TYK 4 adds information system understanding)
Audit plan contents (3)Risk assessment procedures (SA 315); further procedures at assertion level (SA 330); other SA-required procedures."what shall the audit plan include", "sample size"
Audit programme — matters (6)Nature of business; overall plan; internal control and accounting procedures; size and management structure; information on organisation; accounting and management policies."instructions to assistants", "formulating audit programme"

Examiner's twist: a story about a fintech/new venture asks "is involving an IT expert a factor in the strategy?" — Yes: 2.2(i) qualitative resources (experts on complex matters); responsibility not shared (SA 620). The one line that scores: "strategy sets scope, timing and direction; plan sets nature, timing and extent."

Audit programme — first audit in three stages; when to alter

  • First engagement: (i) broad outline; (ii) details filled after reviewing internal and accounting procedures, considering IC deficiencies; (iii) after detailed checking, decide extent of special (first-time/opening balance) procedures — debtors/creditors confirmation, physical inspection of fixed assets and stock.
  • Review at each subsequent engagement for: experience of previous audits; important changes (IC, procedures, management, scope); current year IC evaluation.
  • Alter when: turnover substantially increased or accounting organisation/personnel changed; IC not as effective as assumed; extraordinary increase in book debts or stocks; suspicion of misappropriation.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Audit Planning, Strategy & ExecutionSM Ch 3

SA 600 — Using the Work of Another Auditor (as in SM)

  • Principal auditor reports on entity's financial information that includes components audited by another auditor. Component: division, branch, subsidiary, JV, associate or other entity. SA 600 does not deal with joint auditors or predecessor auditor.
  • Principal remains responsible for his opinion; may rely on others if he exercises adequate skill and care and has no reason to believe he should not. Independent statutory appointment (e.g. branch auditors under Companies Act) → report should expressly state the fact of such reliance.
  • Before acting as principal, consider: materiality of portion he audits; knowledge of components' business; risk of material misstatement in components audited by others; additional procedures giving significant participation.
  • Procedures: other auditor not an ICAI member → consider professional competence; advise the other auditor of use of his work, coordinate at planning (special areas, inter-component transactions, time-table); advise significant accounting, auditing, reporting requirements and obtain representation on compliance; review written summary/questionnaire; consider significant findings; supplementary tests if needed.
  • Documentation: components audited by others, procedures and conclusions; how qualifications in other auditor's report were dealt with. Need not document reasons for limiting procedures where previously satisfied about other auditor's quality control.
  • Reporting: work cannot be used and no sufficient additional procedures → qualified or disclaimer (scope limitation). Other auditor's modified report → consider whether principal's report needs modification.
  • Division of responsibility: principal not responsible for work entrusted to others, except circumstances that should have aroused suspicion; report states extent of components audited by others (TYU 4 — 1,034 bank branches).

SA 610 — Using the Work of Internal Auditors Asked: May 2026 MCQ CS-II Q8

  • External auditor has sole responsibility; not reduced by using IAF work or direct assistance. IAF/internal auditors are not independent of the entity. SA does not apply if there is no IAF.
  • Can IAF work be used? Evaluate (3): organisational status and policies supporting objectivity; level of competence; systematic and disciplined approach incl. quality control. If any fails → shall not use.
  • Work that can be used: testing operating effectiveness of controls; substantive procedures involving limited judgment; observation of inventory counts; tracing transactions through the information system; testing compliance with regulatory requirements; in some cases audits/reviews of non-significant subsidiaries.
  • Use less, do more directly, the more judgment involved; higher assessed RMM (esp. significant risks); less objectivity support; lower competence. Check that EA is still sufficiently involved in aggregate. Communicate planned use to TCWG (SA 260).
  • Using IAF work: discuss planned use; read IAF reports; perform procedures — work properly planned, performed, supervised, reviewed, documented; sufficient evidence for reasonable conclusions; conclusions appropriate and reports consistent with work.
PointUsing work of IAFDirect assistance (internal auditors under EA's direction)Trigger words
MeaningWork already performed by the function, used as evidenceInternal auditors perform audit procedures under direction, supervision and review of EA"assigned to EA's team", "work under our supervision" → direct assistance
GateObjectivity support + competence + systematic approach incl. QCFirst: is it prohibited by law? (if so, consider extension to component auditors). Then evaluate threats to objectivity (incl. inquiry of internal auditors re interests/relationships) and competence"family member in stores", "financial interest", "association with the department"
Shall not use ifStatus/policies don't support objectivity; lacks competence; no systematic approachSignificant threats to objectivity; or lacks sufficient competence
Not forAll significant judgments remain with EASignificant judgments; higher assessed RMM where judgment more than limited; work reported to management/TCWG by IAF; decisions EA makes under SAs"evaluate going concern", "decide sample/ materiality", "significant risk area"
ReviewPerform procedures on adequacy of IAF workDirect, supervise, review per SA 220; recognise lack of independence; check back to underlying evidence for some work

Examiner's twist: case says IA is "well-functioning" and counted inventory, but stock is slow-moving (TYU 5) — the ask is valuation (high judgment, significant risk) → EA performs directly. Or IA head is a non-tech-savvy retired banker appointed on recommendation (opening case) → competence and objectivity doubtful → don't use. Scoring line: "Responsibility not reduced; more judgment / higher risk → less use."

AAA · Twist notes · Audit Planning, Strategy & ExecutionSM Ch 3

SA 620 — Using the Work of an Auditor's Expert Asked: Sep 2025 MCQ CS-III Q10; May 2026 MCQ CS-I Q5; May 2025 Q2(b)

  • Auditor's expert: expertise in a field other than accounting or auditing, work used by the auditor; may be internal (partner/staff incl. temporary, of firm or network firm) or external.
  • SA 620 does not deal with: team member with expertise in a specialised area of accounting/auditing; use of a management's expert (dealt with in SA 500).
  • Sole responsibility not reduced; but if work is adequate, auditor may accept expert's findings as appropriate evidence.
  • Need (5 stages): understanding entity incl. IC; identifying and assessing RMM; overall responses at FS level; further audit procedures at assertion level; evaluating sufficiency and appropriateness of evidence. Auditor may instead gain understanding by experience, education/ professional development, discussion with auditors of similar engagements.
  • Deciding whether to use: management used an expert?; nature, significance, complexity; RMM; expected procedures and availability of alternative evidence.
  • NTE of procedures — consider: nature of matter; RMM; significance of expert's work; previous experience with that expert; whether subject to firm's QC. More extensive when: significant matter with subjective, complex judgments; not previously used / no prior knowledge; procedures integral to the audit rather than advice on an individual matter; external expert not subject to firm's QC.
  • Competence, capabilities, objectivity: for external expert include inquiry about interests and relationships (financial interests, business and personal relationships, other services); inquire of entity; discuss safeguards; written representation may be appropriate. Relevance of specialty (property-casualty actuary vs pensions).
  • Understanding the field enough to (a) determine nature, scope, objectives and (b) evaluate adequacy.
  • Agreement — in writing when appropriate (4): nature, scope, objectives; respective roles and responsibilities; nature, timing, extent of communication incl. form of report; confidentiality. Detailed written agreement needed where: access to sensitive data; highly complex matter; not previously used; greater extent and significance (TYK 8).
Evaluate adequacy (3 tests)What to checkProcedures / factors (SM)
(a) Findings or conclusionsRelevance, reasonableness, consistency with other audit evidenceInquiry of expert; review working papers and reports; corroborative procedures (observe work, published data, third-party confirmation, detailed analytics, re-perform calculations); discuss with another expert; discuss report with management
(b) Significant assumptions and methodsRelevance and reasonableness in the circumstancesGenerally accepted in the field; consistent with FRF; dependence on specialised models; consistent with management's — if not, reasons and effects
(c) Significant source dataRelevance, completeness, accuracyVerify origin of data incl. controls and transmission to expert; review for completeness and internal consistency
  • Not adequate: agree further work with expert, or perform further procedures; unresolved → modified opinion (SA 705).
  • Reference in report: not in an unmodified report unless law/regulation requires (then state it does not reduce responsibility). May refer if relevant to understanding a modification — again state no reduction of responsibility.
PointAuditor's expert (SA 620)Management's expert (SA 500)Trigger words
Who uses the workAuditor, to obtain evidenceEntity, to prepare FS"company got valuation done", "CTO's report", "actuary's certificate accepted by company" → SA 500
Auditor's dutyCompetence, capabilities, objectivity; understand field; agreement; evaluate adequacyTo extent necessary, having regard to significance: (a) evaluate competence, capabilities, objectivity; (b) understand the work; (c) evaluate appropriateness as evidence for the assertion"evaluate appropriateness as audit evidence" → SA 500
ObjectivityInquiry on interests/relationships; written representation possibleEmployee expert: threat always present — not ordinarily more objective than other employees; engaged expert: discuss interests, safeguards"incentives linked to profit", "re-appointed for 5 years"

Examiner's twist: the story builds up a valuation dispute, then asks only "how should the auditor evaluate the assumptions and methods" — write the four factors under (b), not the whole SA. Another trap: a management-engaged expert ("independent") is still a management's expert — SA 500, not SA 620. If the auditor's expert played a significant role in preparing the information audited, he is a management's expert (objectivity threat).

SA 540 — Accounting Estimates incl. Fair Value Asked: Sep 2025 Q3(b); MTP Sep 2025 5(b)

  • Low estimation uncertainty: non-complex business; frequent routine estimates; observable data (published interest rates, exchange-traded prices); simple FV method; well-known model with observable inputs. High: outcome of litigation; FV of unlisted derivatives; entity-developed models or unobservable inputs; wage revision under negotiation.
  • Active market price quotations are ordinarily best evidence of FV — but uncertainty may exist if holding is significant to market or marketability restricted, or market illiquid.
  • Degree of uncertainty varies with: nature of estimate; extent of generally accepted method; subjectivity of assumptions.
  • Management bias: lack of neutrality; susceptibility increases with subjectivity; may be visible only in aggregate or over periods; intention to mislead = fraudulent. Auditor is not responsible for predicting future conditions. Difference between outcome and estimate is not necessarily a misstatement.
  • Objective: sufficient appropriate evidence whether estimates (recognised or disclosed) are reasonable and related disclosures adequate.
  • Understanding (risk assessment): FRF requirements; how management identifies need for estimates (inquire about changes — new transactions, changed terms, policy changes, regulatory changes, new conditions); how management makes estimates — (i) method/model, (ii) relevant controls, (iii) use of expert, (iv) assumptions, (v) change in method and why, (vi) how estimation uncertainty assessed.
  • Review of prior period outcomes (or re-estimation) — required; nature and extent depends on nature of estimates and relevance to current RMM; not intended to call into question prior judgments based on information then available. Gives: effectiveness of management's prior process; evidence for re-estimation; evidence on uncertainty to disclose; indicators of bias. Management refusal is not correct (TYK 7).
  • Identify and assess: evaluate degree of estimation uncertainty; decide whether high-uncertainty estimates give rise to significant risk.
  • Responses (one or more): events up to date of auditor's report; test data and how estimate was made (data accurate, complete, relevant; external data source; recalculate; internal consistency); test controls with substantive procedures; consider need for specialised skills.
For estimates with significant riskAuditor shall evaluate / obtain evidence on
Estimation uncertainty (3)(a) how management considered alternative assumptions or outcomes and why rejected; (b) whether significant assumptions are reasonable; (c) where relevant, management's intent and ability to carry out specific courses of action. Not adequately addressed → develop a range if necessary.
Recognition and measurementDecision to recognise or not; selected measurement basis — per FRF.
DisclosureAdequacy of disclosure of estimation uncertainty — may be inadequate even if FRF-compliant.
DocumentationBasis for conclusions on reasonableness and disclosure; indicators of possible management bias.
  • Written representations: management (and TCWG where appropriate) believe significant assumptions are reasonable.

Examiner's twist: "management says auditor cannot look back at last year's estimates" (real estate/construction) → SA 540 requires the review; it does not question prior judgments. Or a warranty/ decommissioning provision cut by 15% on an internal report → significant risk: write the (a)(b)(c) trio + range + disclosure + representation; then SA 500 for the internal expert.

SA 220 / SQC 1 — Quality (only as in Ch 3)

  • Engagement teams may rely on firm's QC system unless information suggests otherwise. For listed entities, EQCR also considers the team's evaluation of the firm's independence (TYK 5 — EP wrong to withhold independence documentation from EQCR).

SA 520 — Analytical Procedures

  • Substantive AP (4): suitability for assertions (considering RMM and tests of details); reliability of data (source, comparability, nature and relevance, controls over preparation); develop a sufficiently precise expectation; determine acceptable difference without further investigation.
  • Near the end: shall perform AP to form an overall conclusion whether FS are consistent with understanding of entity; may reveal unrecognised RMM → revise assessment (SA 315).
  • Investigating differences: inquire of management and obtain evidence on responses; other procedures if no explanation or explanation inadequate.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Audit Planning, Strategy & ExecutionSM Ch 3 / Ch 4

SA 450 — Evaluation of Misstatements Asked: Jan 2026 Q1(b)

  • Misstatement: difference in amount, classification, presentation or disclosure from FRF; from error or fraud. Uncorrected misstatements: accumulated and not corrected.
  • Accumulate all except clearly trivial. Clearly trivial ≠ "not material" — wholly different (smaller) order; inconsequential by size, nature or circumstances. Any uncertainty → not clearly trivial.
  • Revise strategy/plan if: nature/circumstances indicate other misstatements may exist which with accumulated ones could be material; or aggregate approaches materiality (SA 320). Management corrected a class at auditor's request → additional procedures to see whether misstatements remain.
  • Communicate all accumulated misstatements timely to appropriate level of management (unless prohibited) and request correction. Refusal → understand reasons and consider them in evaluating FS as a whole.
  • Before evaluating uncorrected misstatements: reassess SA 320 materiality in light of actual results. Then decide whether material individually or in aggregate considering (a) size and nature, particular circumstances; (b) effect of prior-period uncorrected misstatements.
  • TCWG (SA 260): uncorrected misstatements and effect on opinion; material ones identified individually; request correction; effect of prior-period ones.
  • Written representation: effects of uncorrected misstatements immaterial individually and in aggregate; summary included in or attached.
  • Documentation (3): clearly-trivial amount; all accumulated misstatements and whether corrected; conclusion on materiality of uncorrected and basis.
TypeMeaning (SM)Trigger words
FactualNo doubt about it"invoice recorded twice", "arithmetical error"
JudgmentalManagement's estimates the auditor considers unreasonable, or policies inappropriate"provision too low in auditor's view", "policy not suitable"
ProjectedAuditor's best estimate of misstatement in a population, projecting sample errors"error in sample of 60 invoices extrapolated"
PointSA 320 materiality (Ch 4 TYK 1, 13, 14)SA 450 evaluationTrigger words
WhenPlanning and performing; set when establishing strategy; revise on becoming aware of information that would have caused a different amount initiallyThroughout and at completion — evaluating effect of identified and uncorrected misstatements"benchmark", "percentage", "estimated sales" → SA 320
Lower revised amountDetermine whether to revise performance materiality and whether NTE of further procedures remain appropriateReassess SA 320 materiality before evaluating uncorrected misstatements"actual results differ from estimate used"
Specific levelsLower materiality for particular classes/balances/disclosures if lesser misstatements could influence users (Deepti & Co. — imported machinery, forex)Size and nature of each misstatement considered"particular transaction", "sensitive disclosure"
Benchmark factorsElements of FS; items users focus on; nature, life-cycle, industry; ownership and financing; volatility of benchmark. PBT % normally higher than revenue %. Regular profits → PBT; losses → revenue or gross profit; public utility → total/net cost; custody of assets → assets.—"loss-making", "financed by debt"

Examiner's twist: MINI Builders type — materiality was set on 11 months extrapolated sales; last month only 30% of estimate. Ask is "how to evaluate uncorrected misstatements" → first reassess materiality (SA 450 + SA 320), then performance materiality and NTE. Students who list SA 450 communication alone lose the main mark.

AAA · Twist notes · Materiality, Risk Assessment & Internal ControlSM Ch 4

04Ch 04 — Materiality, Risk Assessment and Internal Control

Asked: Sep 2025 MCQ CS-III Q9 (control activities — information processing) · Sep 2025 Q6(a) (SA 265 communication) · Jan 2026 MCQ CS-I Q6 (auditor's responsibility on IC) · Jan 2026 Q2(b) (internal check principles, effect on inherent and control risk). Also MTP May 2026 5(b) (circumstances that change risk).

Audit risk and its components

  • Audit risk: risk of expressing an inappropriate opinion when FS are materially misstated. AR = IR × CR × DR; AR = RMM × DR; RMM = IR × CR.
  • Combined IR and CR is inversely related to DR; materiality inversely related to audit risk. Objective: reduce AR to acceptably low level — assess RMM, limit DR.
  • Two levels: FS level (pervasive — incompetent top accountant affects many assertions) and assertion level (payables valuation low vs inventory valuation high).
  • SM TYK 7: IR 40%, controls prevent 75% → CR 25%, RMM 10%; detection satisfaction 60% → DR 40%; AR 4%.
ComponentDefinition keySM examples / commentaryTrigger words
Inherent riskSusceptibility of an assertion to material misstatement assuming no related controlsComplex calculations; high-value inventory; estimates with measurement uncertainty; lack of working capital; declining industry; technological obsolescence"nature of business", "product may become obsolete", "foreign funds / political NGO"
Control riskEntity's IC will not prevent, or detect and correct on a timely basisEntity-level controls (board oversight, ITGC, HR) pervasive; activity-level relate to assertions; some CR always exists"no rotation", "same person purchases and pays", "delegation not followed"
Detection riskAuditor will not detect a material misstatementInappropriate procedure; misapplying; misinterpreting results; inverse to RMM"first-year audit", "inappropriate sampling", "time constraint"
  • Assertions (SA 315): transactions — occurrence, completeness, accuracy, cut-off, classification; balances — existence, rights and obligations, completeness, valuation and allocation; presentation and disclosure — occurrence and rights and obligations, completeness, classification and understandability, accuracy and valuation.
  • Steps for risk identification (TYK 11): assess significance and revise materiality for specific balance; likelihood; document assertions affected; impact on each assertion; identify significant risks needing separate response; enquire and document management's response; consider IC (routine/periodic, prevent/detect, manual/automated); unique characteristics; particular inherent characteristics (high-value inventory, complex contracts, no paper trail, single large customer).

Risk-based audit — three phases

  • Risk assessment: acceptance/continuance; planning; risk assessment procedures (inquiries, inspection, observation); identify controls and assess design and implementation; assess RMM; identify significant risks and risks where substantive procedures alone are not sufficient; communicate material weaknesses in design/implementation to management and TCWG.
  • Risk response: consider assertions not addressable by substantive procedures alone (highly automated processing); controls that if tested reduce substantive work; substantive analytics; unpredictability; management override; specific procedures for significant risks. Before conclusion, confirm control risk assessment; deviations → revise and modify substantive procedures.
  • Reporting: change in assessed risk? conclusions appropriate? suspicious circumstances?
  • Delegation of powers violated in 5 of 10 claims (TYU 1) → controls not operating → revise CR, RMM, and NTE of substantive procedures.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Materiality, Risk Assessment & Internal ControlSM Ch 4

Internal control — definition, tests of control, scope, limitations

  • SA 315 definition: process designed, implemented and maintained by TCWG, management and other personnel for reasonable assurance on reliability of financial reporting, effectiveness and efficiency of operations, safeguarding of assets, compliance with laws.
  • Tests of control needed to support any CR assessment less than high; the lower the CR, the more evidence. Consider how applied, consistency during the period and by whom. Observation evidence relates only to the point in time. Different controls at different times → consider each; breakdown for a portion → separate consideration.
  • Scope extends to administrative controls; auditor evaluates only those bearing on reliability of financial records.
  • Limitations: cost vs benefit; not directed at unusual transactions; human error; collusion; abuse/override by responsible person; management manipulation of transactions or estimates. Gives reasonable, not absolute assurance.
Look-alike listItemsTrigger words → this list
Objectives of IC relating to accounting system (4)Executed per general/specific authorisation; promptly recorded; assets and records safeguarded from unauthorised access; assets verified at intervals and discrepancies acted on"objectives of internal control"
Basic accounting control objectives (7)All transactions recorded; real; properly valued; recorded timely; properly posted; properly classified and disclosed; properly summarised"limit account balance tests" (TYK 5)
Structure of IC (5)Segregation of duties; authorisation; adequacy of records and documents; accountability and safeguarding of assets; independent checks"policies and procedures to achieve objectives"
Components of IC (5)Control environment; entity's risk assessment process; control activities; information system and communication; monitoring of controls"components", "framework" (TYK 2)
Elements of control environment (7)Integrity and ethical values; commitment to competence; participation by TCWG; management's philosophy and operating style; organisational structure; assignment of authority and responsibility; HR policies"tone at the top", "integrity", "whistle blower oversight"
Control activities (4 categories)Performance reviews; information processing (application controls and general IT controls); physical controls; segregation of duties"budget vs actual", "edit checks", "counts compared with records"
Information system — methods and records (5)Identify and record valid transactions; describe timely for classification; measure value; determine time period; present properly"infrastructure, software, people, procedures, data"
MonitoringOngoing/separate evaluations; management review of bank reconciliations; internal auditors on sales terms; legal department oversight; customer complaints, regulator communications"whether controls continue to operate over time"
Control activitySM examplesDo not confuse with
