Examiner's twist: May 2024 Q4(b) (CA Lalit) asked three things: key issues, difference from assurance engagements, and SQC 1. The SQC 1 line is short and certain — "SQC 1 applies to all Engagement Standards; SRS 4410 is one, so it applies". The SM case also shows SA 220 is not the answer for a compilation.

Beyond the SM (practical): banks often ask for "certified" statements prepared by the CA; if the CA only compiled them, the report must make the no-assurance position clear.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Digital Auditing and AssuranceSM Ch 12

12Ch 12 — Digital Auditing and Assurance

Asked: Sep 2025 Q4(b) remote audit confidentiality (5M) · Jan 2026 MCQ DoS attack, Q3(c) RPA audit implications and risks (4M) · May 2026 MCQ whaling/smishing/vishing, Q2(c) SA 315 entity's risk assessment for cyber risks (4M) · MTP: risks from use of IT, impact-of-technology considerations, cyber-risk impact areas.

1. Digital Audit vs 2. Auditing Digitally (SM p 12.4–12.10)

  • Digital audit = placing assurance on the effectiveness of the IT systems implemented in an organisation (auditor audits the client's technology).
  • Auditing digitally = using advancements in technology for conducting an effective and efficient audit (auditor uses technology: sampling tools, bot for statutory payments compliance).
  • SM example (p 12.8): manager's weekly manual review of vendor master changes re-performed by a BOT — logs in, generates report, selects samples, pulls approvals from SharePoint, flags changes without approval, summarises in Excel; auditor reviews only the exceptions file.
List (SM)ItemsTrigger words → this list
Key features of digital audit (1.2)Encourages technology advancement · more reliable audit report (quality of opinion) · savings in time, cost, human effort (24x7 processes) · standardised processes · end-to-end understanding · future digital strategy (AI, robotics) · informed decisions"what does a digital audit offer the auditee/organisation"
Advantages of digital audit (1.3)Enhanced effectiveness & efficiency · better audit quality · lower costs · better analytics · improved risk assessment (5)"benefits", "advantages" of digital audit
Considerations / challenges (1.4)Know business benefits wanted · think people first (change is difficult) · target the right processes · automation not stand-alone, part of digital strategy · standardise before automating (bots don't adapt to change) · governance and data security in the risk framework"challenges", "before automating", "bots failing after process change"
Advantages of auditing digitally (2.2)Improved quality (sample → full population) · decreasing human dependency · increases transparency (audit trail in ERPs: date, who, what changed) · automation and ease (dashboards e.g. Power BI) · improved efficiency (talent retention) · better risk assessment (6)"auditor uses tools", "full population", "audit trail feature"
Considerations in auditing digitally (2.3)What problems are you solving? · Which technology can help? · How will you upskill people? · Range of automated solutions"questions the audit firm should ask before buying a tool"
  • Areas of focus on new technology (1.4): new activities / changes in processes (new revenue streams, roles, automation of manual tasks, staffing affecting control environment) · changes in how systems are developed and maintained (new risks → new controls) · impact on how the entity obtains/generates and uses relevant, quality information for internal control.
Automation range (2.3.4)What it is (SM)Trigger words
Macros and scriptsRules-based automation within a specific application"Excel macro", single application
Business Process Automation (BPA)Re-engineering existing business processes, e.g. workflows"redesigned approval workflow"
Robotic Process Automation (RPA)Labour-intensive, repetitive activities across multiple systems and interfaces"bot logs in to ERP and portal", repetitive
Intelligent Process Automation (IPA)RPA + AI to identify patterns, learn over time, optimise workflows"learns", "improves over time"

Examiner's twist: a story about the auditor using a bot is "auditing digitally"; a story about the auditor reviewing the client's ERP controls is "digital audit". An "intelligent bot that learns" is IPA, not RPA. Score with the one-line definition first.

3. Understand the IT environment (SM p 12.10–12.13) MTP Jan 2026 S-I 3(c)

  • Stages: Understand → Identify → Assess. Understanding how the entity relies on IT is required as per SA 315; complexity drives whether to involve IT specialists/experts.
  • Understanding of automated environment includes: applications used · IT infrastructure components per application (database, OS, network, server/storage) · organisation structure and governance · policies, procedures, processes · extent of IT integration, use of service organisations · IT risks and controls.
  • Key areas (3.1) (TYK 16): (1) flow of transactions (changes via program changes or direct data changes in databases) · (2) significant systems · (3) manual and automated controls · (4) technologies used (blockchain incl. crypto, robotics, AI, IoT, biometrics, drone — may need specialists) · (5) complexity of IT environment.
  • Complexity factors: automation used · reliance on system-generated reports · customisation · business model · significant changes during the year · emerging technologies.

4. Risks arising from use of IT (SM p 12.13–12.14) MTP Nov 2026 S-I 2(c)

  • More IT risks when volume or complexity of automated application controls is higher and management places greater reliance on them. Risks may also relate to cybersecurity.
  • SM list: unauthorised access to data (destruction/improper changes, unauthorised or non-existent transactions; multiple users on a common database) · IT personnel with excess access privileges (breaks SoD); unauthorised changes to master files · unauthorised changes to IT applications · failure to make necessary updates · inappropriate manual intervention · data loss or corruption · system downtime (hardware, configuration, cyberattack, power) · system integration and compatibility (different versions of same software, unapplied patches) · regulatory compliance risk. Also performance issues (solved by scaling — expensive).

Examiner's twist (MTP pattern): each fact maps to one SM risk — change in live environment without approval/testing → unauthorised changes to IT applications; branches on old software version → integration/compatibility; manual override without review → inappropriate manual intervention; ransomware + weak backup → data loss/corruption. Name the SM risk heading, then tie the fact.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Digital Auditing and AssuranceSM Ch 12

4.3 IT dependencies (SM p 12.14–12.16) — TYK 3, 12

  • Arise when IT is used to initiate, authorise, record, process or report transactions or other financial data for inclusion in FS.
  • Why identify: shows reliance on IT, how IT is integrated into business model, potential IT risks, related ITGCs, and an effective and efficient audit approach.
  • Auditor should scope in ITGCs when IT dependencies are identified; if ITGCs not implemented/operating effectively → IT dependencies cannot be relied upon.
IT dependency (5)SM meaning and exampleTrigger words
Automated controlsEnforce business rules: PO approval via workflow, format check (date format), existence check (no duplicate customer number), reasonableness check (maximum payment)"system rejects", "cannot enter duplicate"
ReportsSystem-generated; used in manual controls or by auditor for sample selection, TOD, SAP (vendor master report, customer ageing)"ageing report used for provision"
CalculationsAccounting procedure done by system: straight-line depreciation; price × quantity invoicing"system computes"
SecurityAccess restriction incl. segregation of duties"roles", "user rights"
InterfacesProgrammed logic transferring data between systems (payroll sub-ledger → GL)"data flows from X to Y system"

ITGC areas — objective and controls (SM p 12.16)

ITGC areaObjective (SM)Controls (SM)
Access securityAccess to programs and data is authenticated and authorised to meet FR objectivesAccess requests reviewed/authorised · terminated users removed timely · periodic review of access rights · admin and sensitive generic IDs monitored · security policies maintained · OS and database access restricted
Program changeModified systems continue to meet FR objectivesChange policy · dev/test/production segregated · changes tracked · tested and approved before migration to production · emergency changes approved · SoD between developer and implementor
Data centre and network operationsProduction systems appropriately backed up to meet FR objectivesBackup/recovery policy · data backed up and recoverable · restoration testing · SLA monitoring · batch jobs monitored, access restricted
Look-alikeITGCAutomated (application) control
LevelOver the IT environment (access, change, operations)Within an application, enforcing a business rule at transaction entry
SM examplesRemoving terminated user; testing changes before production; restoration testingFormat / existence / reasonableness checks; workflow PO approval
LinkITGCs address risks from IT dependencies; if ITGCs fail, automated controls, reports, calculations and interfaces cannot be relied upon. SM 7: scope may include "testing general IT and application controls" of peripheral systems.
Trigger"live environment changed", "ex-employee ID active", "backup never restored""system did not block", "duplicate invoice accepted"

Examiner's twist: question narrates a wrong depreciation figure and asks "which IT dependency" — answer calculation, not automated control. A payroll-to-GL mismatch is an interface. Auditor's next step is always: test the related ITGC (program change for calculation logic).

5. Cyber risk — attack types (SM p 12.16–12.20) Jan 2026 MCQ (DoS) · May 2026 MCQ (whaling/smishing/vishing)

  • Cyber-attack: attempt to gain unauthorised access to a computing system or network with intent to cause damage, steal, expose, alter, disable or destroy data.
AttackDistinguishing key (SM)Trigger words
RansomwareEncrypts data; decryption key for payment; via phishing links, unpatched vulnerabilities"files locked, ransom"
Fileless malwareUses native, legitimate tools; no code installed; hard to detect"no file found, built-in tools misused"
TrojanAppears legitimate (free download, OS program); installed via social engineering"free utility downloaded"
Mobile malwareTargets mobile devices (malicious downloads, unsecured Wi-Fi)"phone app"
DoSFloods network with false requests; users can't access email/websites; usually no data lost, no ransom"website down, flood of requests"
Spear phishingTargets specific individuals or organisations, typically email"named finance staff"
WhalingTargets senior / C-level executives"Chairman", "CFO"
SmishingText messages (SMS)"SMS link"
VishingPhone calls / voice messages"caller from bank"
Domain spoofingFake website or email domain with subtle differences"look-alike URL"
Email spoofingForged sender address"from-address faked"
Identity-basedValid user's credentials compromised; same ID/password reused across accounts"reused password"
Insider threatCurrent or former employees with access and process knowledge"ex-employee"
DNS tunnellingDNS queries/responses used to bypass security and extract data bit by bit"DNS traffic"
IoT-basedTargets IoT device/network; hijack, steal data, join infected group"smart camera hacked"

Examiner's twist: SM's own example (p 12.19) labels a phishing email to the CEO as "Spear Fishing", while the whaling definition targets C-level executives; ICAI's May 2026 key treated an email to the Chairman as whaling. If the option list has whaling and the target is a C-level executive, choose whaling; if only spear phishing is offered, choose it. Write the definition you rely on.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Digital Auditing and AssuranceSM Ch 12

5.2 Stages of cyber risk (SM p 12.20–12.21) MTP May 2026 S-I 2(c)

StageSM contentTrigger words
1 AssessingNo one immune; consider at least: ransomware disabling the organisation (incl. plants) · criminals using phishing and hacks for fraud and theft · insiders (malicious or accidental)"what threats should the entity consider"
2 Impact (indicative areas)Regulatory costs · business interruption · data loss, reputational loss, litigation · ransomware · IP theft (impairment charge) · incident response cost (investigation, remediation) · breach of privacy · fines and penalties (8)"identify areas of impact", "consequences"
3 ManagingHolistic understanding of threats · assess IT/cyber programme against regulation · align cyber and IT initiatives with strategy and critical risks · understand accepted risks and documented compensating controls"strategic approach"
  • TYU 1: cyber risk is a business risk, not IT alone. Even attacks not aimed at financial systems can let attackers manipulate financial records, modify automated business rules, modify automated controls relied on by management → auditor considers whether it is a risk of material misstatement.

5.3 Cybersecurity framework (SM p 12.21–12.24) May 2026 Q2(c)

FunctionPurpose (SM)Categories (SM)Must-write auditor points
IdentifyOrganisational understanding to manage cyber riskAsset management · business environment · governance · risk assessment and strategyWhether entity's risk assessment process considers cyber risk; periodic assessment; inventory of information assets reviewed periodically; classify/prioritise by sensitivity; governance review of effect on ICFR; risk-based programme (NIST, ISO); roles CISO, CIO; discuss with TCWG
ProtectSafeguards for critical servicesAccess control · awareness and training · data security · information protection processesMonitor unauthorised access to electronic assets; formal training; identify material digital assets (IP, patents)
DetectIdentify occurrence of an eventAnomalies and events · continuous monitoring · detection processesAnti-virus, firewall logs monitored, number of denied events, updates
RespondAct on detected eventResponse planning (incident response plan) · communications · analysis · mitigation and improvementsCapture nature of incident and how identified; communicate to TCWG; assess litigation, regulatory investigation, remediation costs
RecoverResilience; restore impaired servicesRecovery planning · improvements · communicationsRecovery plan after impact communicated to regulators; patches, better controls, firewall

Examiner's twist: "SA 315 — evaluate entity's risk assessment process for cyber risk" = the Identify function only (asset inventory, classification, governance/ICFR, NIST/ISO, CISO/CIO, TCWG). Writing detect/respond points scores little. Stages (assess–impact–manage) ≠ framework functions (identify–protect–detect–respond–recover).

  • Hotel case (p 12.25): CEO clicked a phishing link → wire fraud of more than ₹1 million, not recovered. Lessons: staff email training · stringent wire-transfer protocols with MFA · cyber incident response plan ready.

5.4 Control considerations for cyber risks (SM p 12.25–12.27)

Control areaWhy (SM)Auditor's questions (SM)
Vendor set-up and modificationsPhishing requests to change vendor bank details → funds paid to fraudsters, liability to real vendor wrongly reducedWho changes vendor master; centralised?; email used (MFA on email?); systems used; authentication (call-back, MFA)
Electronic transfer of fundsFraudulent wire-transfer requests, incl. ones appearing to come from financial institutionsStaff educated on scams; call-back / dual authentication; systems for request, authorisation, release
Patch managementAttacks exploit known vulnerabilities from unapplied patchesPatch programme; periodic vulnerability scans; how vendors notify patches

5.5 Remote audit (SM p 12.27–12.29) Sep 2025 Q4(b) · TYK 10, 15

  • Partially or completely virtual; auditor uses technology to obtain evidence or review documentation with auditee participation.
  • Feasibility and planning: timelines, meeting platform, data exchange mechanism, access authorisation requests; competencies and resources; evidence via document-sharing platform.
  • Confidentiality, security, data protection: restricted platform access; encrypt data sent across the network; remove information from platform once reviewed and archive as per standards; consider laws (no recording of sound/images without agreement); no screenshots of auditees; screenshots of documents only if previously authorised by auditee; use VPN to access auditee's IT system.
  • Risk assessment: clear communication; assess and document for each audit whether remote audit is sufficient, involving all team members and auditee representative.
  • Controls to test: remote access approved; encrypted transfer; list of remote workers reviewed; VPN only; VPN access terminated timely when employee leaves or audit completes.
Advantages (SM)Disadvantages (SM)
Cost and time effective (no travel) · comfort and flexibility · evidence gathering spread over weeks, not taking personnel off daily work · first-hand evidence directly from IT system · wider choice of global expertsNetwork interruptions · can't see facility culture, body language; time zones · more opportunity for doctored documents and omissions · security/confidentiality violation · remote access may not be allowed · cultural challenges, local law knowledge · physical verification and stock taking cannot be performed
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Digital Auditing and AssuranceSM Ch 12

6. Data analytics and CAATs (SM p 12.29–12.32) — TYU 2, TYK 6

  • Data analytics: generating meaningful information from raw system data using processes, tools, techniques. Audit analytics: analysing large data sets for insights, trends, conclusions. Helps: discover patterns · identify anomalies · extract other useful information.
  • Data analytics methods used in an audit = CAATs. Examples of emerging technologies named: data analytics (CAATs: ACL, Alteryx, Power BI, CaseWare), AI, RPA, blockchain.
CAAT toolSM key useSM illustration → trigger
ACLData extraction and analysis; fraud detection, risk managementGL dump vs system TB reconciliation beyond Excel capacity (record count, sum, pivot) → "data too big for Excel"
AlteryxConsolidate data to assess controls; workflow audit trail; no coding needed; ML for fraud patterns; automate reconciliations, consolidations, continuous auditLogistics revenue recomputed for all entries — old rates used as addendum not updated → "re-computation of entire population"
Power BIBI visualisation for non-technical users; outliers; interactive dashboards to managementApparel sales during non-business hours → "trend, outlier, dashboard"
CaseWareConduct assurance engagements; documents into client-ready reports; real-time client link; same documents for all authorised users"audit file, review and compilation on screen"
CAAT test (TYU 2 answer)SM example
Identify exceptionsCash transactions above ₹10,000
Identify errorsAccount number not numeric
Verify calculationsTDS rate applied as per criteria
Existence of recordsFields with null values — invoices without vendor name
Data completenessNull values in key fields (date, invoice number, value, name)
Data consistencyInvoices not in required sequence
Duplicate paymentsRelationship between tables — duplicate payment for same invoice
Accounts exceeding authorised limitUser beyond authorised limit; payment beyond amount due; overdraft beyond limit

Examiner's twist: "invoices not in sequence" is data consistency, not completeness; "cash above ₹10,000" is identify exceptions, not authorised limit. SM overlaps "existence of records" (invoices without vendor name) with "completeness" (null name in key fields) — quote the SM example you rely on. The SM does not classify analytics into descriptive/diagnostic/predictive/prescriptive; answer with the SM's CAAT test headings.

7. Automated tools — IoT, AI, blockchain, NFT, RPA (SM p 12.32–12.41) Jan 2026 Q3(c) RPA · TYK 7, 9, 11, 13

  • Use of technology may require scope to include peripheral systems and testing general IT and application controls of those systems.
ToolAudit implications (SM)Common risks (SM)
IoT (components: data collection, analytics, connectivity, people and process)Cannot rely only on manual controls; scope new systems; upskill to evaluate automated controls; new payment paths via service providers — consider volume and controlsDevice hijacking · data siphoning · DoS · data breaches · device theft
AIFocus on logical flow of processes; check unintended bias; effectiveness of algorithms; output reviewed and approved; cybersecurity/bugs; effect on flows and reports; is AI making decisions; if management shifts oversight to AI — what shifted, new risks, risks losing attentionSecurity (more data sources = more entry points) · inappropriate configuration · data privacy (explicit consent)
Blockchain (decentralised, distributed, encrypted ledger)Governance and security of transactions; insecure APIs with legacy systems; data confidentiality; weak development protocols; cross-border data privacy laws → non-complianceInability to reverse transactions / access without keys (lock-out, need contingency plans) · node exposure to cyber-attack · data management compliance · evolving regulation
NFTCode review for token safety, valid contract, data privacy, cyber threatOwnership and copyright · security · narrow market · online frauds
RPAUnderstand RPA processes (data extraction, aggregation, sanitisation, cleansing) — else cannot initiate audit; may need source code review; understand tools to review logs, configuration controls, privileged access; general IT controls applicable as alwaysOperational and execution (no operating model, wrong tool, shortcuts) · change management (lifecycle not followed, incomplete testing) · RPA strategy (wrong expectations, improper KPIs, unrealistic goals)
  • Blockchain audit steps (XY Bank on-chain Nostro case, GIFT City, 24×7 settlement): understand programme · review agreements · identify technology and smart contracts · assess internal controls · review security (encryption, key management) · test transaction validity, reconcile on-chain Nostro with XY Bank accounts · compliance (CDD, monitoring) · BCP/DR · report.
  • RPA hiring (TYU 3): advertise → career site pulls data → algorithm scans → online games → video interview. Benefits: less effort, wider assessment, less recruiter bias, lower cost, higher hire yield, less time to hire, more diversity.

Examiner's twist: "Audit implications" ≠ "risks". For RPA, implications are about the auditor's understanding (processes, source code, logs, ITGC); risks are the three named categories. A blockchain story will tempt "no need to test controls"; SM says blockchain shifts emphasis to tests of controls over tests of details and needs governance and security review.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Digital Auditing and AssuranceSM Ch 12

8. Control considerations of auditing digitally (SM p 12.42–12.43) — TYK 8, 14

  • Three control considerations: (1) holistic understanding of industry and IT changes to evaluate management's process for initiating, processing, recording, then design procedures · (2) risks from new technologies vs legacy systems · (3) need for digital upskilling or specialists (cybersecurity control experts, IT specialists).
  • Technology risks to test controls for: inaccurate processing / processing inaccurate data · unauthorised access (common database) · IT personnel excess privileges (insufficient SoD) · unauthorised/erroneous master file changes · unauthorised program changes · failure to make necessary changes · inappropriate manual intervention · loss of data / inability to access · third-party service providers · cybersecurity.
  • Key steps in a changing technology environment (8.1) MTP Jan 2026 S-I 3(c): professional skepticism on management's risk assessment for new systems · direct and indirect effects on overall risk assessment · impact on flow of transactions, completeness of in-scope ICFR systems, audit response · appropriateness of management's processes to select, develop, operate, maintain technology controls.
Look-alikeItemsTrigger words
Risks from use of IT (4.2)Includes downtime, integration/compatibility, regulatory, performance"risks while planning the audit from use of IT"
Technology risks (8)Includes inaccurate processing and third-party service providers"risks of a new digital system and control considerations"
Control considerations (8)Holistic understanding · new vs legacy · upskilling/specialists"what should the auditor consider to address technology risk"
Key steps (8.1)Skepticism · direct/indirect effects · flow of transactions · management's processes"obtaining an understanding of impact of technology on business, IC and FR"

9. Next Generation Audit (SM p 12.43–12.47)

  • Human-led, tech-powered, data-driven. Technologies: 3D printing, AR, VR, biotechnology, drones (UAV), quantum.
  • Drones: stock counts at remote locations; photograph and examine large quantities of fixed assets and inventory; combine with QR readers, bar scanners, manual counts.
  • AR adds elements to the real world (Pokémon Go); VR replaces the real world entirely; Metaverse = 3-D digital space combining crypto, AI, AR, VR; considerations: digital monetary systems, governance, identity (KYC), synchronicity. Risks: public safety, cybersecurity, data privacy, lack of standards; taxation, jurisdiction, customer protection.

Examiner's twist: "inventory at 40 remote salt pans" → drones, combined with manual counts; but under remote audit physical verification cannot be performed — do not confuse the two.

Beyond the SM (practical): the audit-trail (edit log) requirement for accounting software under the Companies Act is not covered in this SM chapter; answer Ch 12 questions on audit trail only with the SM line on ERP audit-trail features.

AAA · Twist notes · SDG and ESG AssuranceSM Ch 18

18Ch 18 — SDG and ESG Assurance

Asked: Sep 2025 MCQ BRSR assurance methodology steps, Q4(c) ESG Social pillar elements (4M) · Jan 2026 MCQ NGRBC principle not adhered (P3), Q4(c) IR capitals: human and manufactured (4M) · May 2026 MCQ P7 essential vs leadership indicators, Q4(c) BRSR Principle 6 core elements (4M) · May 2024 Q4(c) Principle 1.

1. Sustainability and ESG pillars (SM p 18.3–18.5) Sep 2025 Q4(c)

  • Sustainable development: meets needs of the present without compromising future generations (Brundtland report). Environment is an exhaustible resource.
  • E: climate policies, energy, waste, pollution, natural resource conservation, treatment of animals. S: relationships and reputation with people and institutions in communities and value chain; labour relations, diversity, inclusion. G: internal practices, controls, procedures to govern itself, invest, comply, meet stakeholder needs.
  • ESG reporting may be qualitative (strategy/policy), quantitative (metrics, KPIs) — mixed approach adds maximum value.
PillarThemes (SM chart)Elements under each theme
EnvironmentClimate change · natural resources · pollution and waste · environment opportunityCarbon emissions, product carbon footprint, financing environmental impact, climate change vulnerability · water stress, biodiversity and land use, raw material sourcing · toxic emission/waste, packaging, e-waste · clean tech, green building, renewable energy
SocialHuman capital · product liability · stakeholders opposition · social opportunityLabour management, health and safety, human capital development, supply chain labour standards · product safety and quality, chemical safety, financial product safety, privacy and data security, responsible investment · controversial sourcing · access to communication, finance, health care, opportunities in nutrition and health
GovernanceCorporate governance · corporate behaviour"Broad" (board) diversity, executive pay, ownership, accounting · business ethics, anti-competitive practices, corruption and instability, financial system stability, tax transparency

Examiner's twist: "Privacy and data security" sits under Social → product liability, not governance. "Tax transparency" is Governance → corporate behaviour. "Controversial sourcing" is Social (stakeholder opposition), not Environment's "raw material sourcing".

2–3. SDGs and global frameworks (SM p 18.6–18.9)

  • 17 SDGs adopted in 2015 by all UN member states; 2030 Agenda; DSDG in UNDESA supports. Corporates contribute through innovation and solutions.

7.1.1 Nine principles and core elements (SM p 18.18–18.23) May 2026 Q4(c) P6 · Jan 2026 MCQ P3 · May 2024 Q4(c) P1

  • SM: two of nine in Environment, three in Social, four in Governance; interlinked.
PrincipleCore elements — must-write (SM)Trigger words → this principle
P1 Ethics, transparency, accountabilityDecisions open to disclosure and accessible to interested parties · governing structure develops policies so ethics not compromised · information on policies and performance to stakeholders · extra care for transparent disclosure of adverse effects · encourage value chain to adopt · proactively respond to outside entities (suppliers, distributors, sub-contractors, regulatory officers) violating principles"anti-bribery", "decisions open to disclosure", "corruption complaints"
P2 Safe and sustainable goods and servicesDesign production methods to minimise resource use · educate consumers on their rights · reduce over-exploitation; reduce, reuse, recycle"product design", "R&D to remove toxic products"
P3 Well-being of all employees incl. value chainRegulatory compliance for employees · dignity; freedom of association, unions, collective bargaining · no child, bonded, involuntary labour · work-life balance · timely wages · living wages · safe, hygienic workplace · skill/career development · free of harassment and violence"contract workers unpaid", "overtime", "union barred"
P4 Stakeholders' interests and responsivenessTransparent communication on impacts, no ambiguity · systematically determine context and identify interested parties · fairly share benefits"identify stakeholders", "consultation"
P5 Human rightsUnderstand rights per Constitution, national laws, International Bill of Human Rights · integrate into management systems · respect rights of communities, consumers, vulnerable and marginalised groups"human rights due diligence"
P6 Protect and restore environmentPolicies to assess and rectify impacts across whole life cycle · optimum use of natural and man-made resources with stakeholder feedback · measure pollution, deforestation, waste, energy, land use · climate resilience per Paris Agreement and National Action Plans for Climate Change · compare with industry best practice to reduce, reuse, recycle/recover · seek avenues to improve"landfill", "coal power", "emissions" (May 2026 MK Steel)
P7 Public and regulatory policyCore elements met holistically in policy formulation/advocacy · use trade groups and industry chambers · advocacy encourages fair competition, prevents human rights abuse"chamber membership", "inputs on draft bill"
P8 Inclusive growth, equitable developmentIdentify impacts on social, cultural, economic aspects (land acquisition, construction) · track adverse impacts, action plans · products for marginalised · CSR aligned to local priorities · avoid displacement; else informed negotiation, fair compensation · respect IP and traditional knowledge, share benefits"land acquisition", "displacement", "CSR"
P9 Value to consumers responsiblyReduce negative impacts · no restriction of freedom of choice, fair competition · disclose adverse impacts · customer data privacy · inform safe use, recycling, disposal, avoid over-consumption · no misleading advertising · grievance redressal · universal access for essential services (utilities), incl. discontinued customers"labels", "barcode authenticity", "misleading ad", "customer data"

Examiner's twist: the agrochemical case (SM p 18.28) shows one fact can hit several principles — R&D removing toxic products = P2; bar-coded user-friendly labels = P9; workers no longer exposed = P3; soil protected = P6. When the ask is "the most relevant principle", pick one and write its core elements; when it is "principles complied with", map each fact. Waste + coal energy = P6, not P2.

7.1.3 Assurance in BRSR; SSAE 3000; SAE 3410 (SM p 18.24–18.25)

  • SEBI started with top 1,000 listed; others expected to follow. BRSR expected to be the single means of sustainability disclosure → assurance more critical. ESG audit evaluates environmental and social risks of products, services, operations; also supply-chain risks, risk management, transparency.
  • SSAE 3000 (ICAI) — Assurance Engagements on Sustainability Information: umbrella standard for all assurance engagements on sustainability information; covers reasonable or limited assurance. Intended users: assurance providers · entities seeking a professional auditor · regulators, investors, other users.
  • Effective: voluntary for periods ending 31 March 2023; mandatory for periods ending on or after 31 March 2024.
  • SAE 3410 — Assurance Engagements on Greenhouse Gas Statements.

7.1.4 Methodology to provide assurance on BRSR (SM p 18.25) Sep 2025 MCQ Q13

  1. Preliminary review of ESG report, parameters
  2. On-site assessment / verification of ESG report
  3. Submission of findings of the on-site assessment and document review
  4. Review of the responses and clarifications on the findings
  5. Preparation of assessment / verification report including final results of assessment / recommendation
  6. Issuance of assessment report and assessment statement

Examiner's twist: MCQs shuffle these six steps. Anchors: review comes first, issuance last; "submission of findings" comes before "review of responses" (you can't review responses to findings not yet given). The SM gives this as a flowchart; the order above follows the arrows' logic.

  • Issued by SRSB: guidance for independent impact assessment of social enterprises; set minimum requirements; laws may add more.

8. Auditor's role — climate-related risks in an FS audit (SM p 18.27) — TYK 6

  • Objective unchanged: reasonable assurance that FS are free from material misstatement.
  • Include climate-related risks in understanding the entity; more relevant for banks and insurance, energy, transportation, materials and buildings, agriculture, food, forestry.
  • User pressure for transparency on climate in auditor's reports — but report must follow applicable SAs. Possible Emphasis of Matter for disclosures of fundamental importance. First determine whether entity has appropriately disclosed climate information under the framework (Ind AS / AS).
  • Read other information for consistency with FS and with public communications (management narratives, press releases, investor updates) — SA 720.
  • Agrochemical case: Company B (DDT, red-label; regulator investigation) → SA 250 (non-compliance may cause fines, litigation, material misstatement) and SA 570 (claims the company may not be able to satisfy). Company A's 8% revenue fall = transitory, not a concern. Both top 1,000 listed → BRSR mandatory.

Examiner's twist: a story full of ESG facts asking about the statutory auditor: the answer is SA 315 understanding, SA 250, SA 570, EOM if disclosures fundamental, SA 720 for BRSR/annual report narratives — not assurance on BRSR. The FS auditor does not certify the ESG numbers by reason of the FS audit.

Beyond the SM (practical): "BRSR Core" and its assurance glide path are not in this SM chapter; for ICAI answers, use the SM's methodology steps and SSAE 3000 points above.

AAA · Twist notes · Due Diligence, Investigation & Forensic AccountingSM Ch 17

17Ch 17 — Due Diligence, Investigation & Forensic Accounting

Asked: Sep 2025 Q6(c) OR fraud investigation, commission scheme (4m) · Jan 2026 Q6(a) steps in conducting due diligence (5m) · May 2026 MCQ CS-III Q12 forensic investigation vs statutory audit · May 2026 Q6(b) investigating accountant, fixed assets (5m). Also Nov 2024 3(c) suppliers' ledger fraud; MTP Sep 2025 credit manager's due diligence.

1. Due diligence — meaning and where used (p.17.2–17.4) Asked: MTP Sep 2025 S-II 4(b)

  • Used to investigate and evaluate a business opportunity; verify agreements, encumbrances on assets, title and ownership, intellectual property, health, safety and environment laws. Implies a general duty to exercise care in any transaction.
  • Legal meaning (write verbatim): "a measure of prudence activity, or assiduity, as is properly to be expected from, and ordinarily exercised by, a reasonable and prudent person under the particular circumstance, not measured by any absolute standard but depends on the relative facts of the special case."
  • A process of investigation performed by investors into a potential investment (operations, management, verification of material facts); timely, sufficient and accurate disclosure of all material information; covers financial as well as non-financial possibilities. Analysis before acquiring a controlling interest to see that the business conforms with what has been presented; also for recommending an investment or advancing a loan/credit.
  • Needed in: corporate restructuring, venture capital financing, lending, leveraged buyouts, public offerings, disinvestment, corporatisation; also when a unit stays in the group but passes from one management team to another. Emerging: mandatory human rights and environmental due diligence — OECD Due Diligence Guidance for Responsible Business Conduct: risk-based due diligence to identify, prevent, mitigate and account for adverse impacts on people, society and the planet.
  • Can be performed by anyone — not an exclusive CA activity (p.17.2; TYK 6 answer p.17.81).

Examiner's twist: A bank's credit manager checks promoters' history, shareholding, past results and projections, SWOT, directors' net worth, CIBIL scores, RBI defaulter list and makes discreet inquiries. Ask: "identify the activity; would a non-CA change the answer; name three other areas." Score: due diligence (define as above) · no difference — any person can do it, as it is prudence and a general duty of care · areas: corporate restructuring, venture capital financing, public offerings. Jotting strengths/weaknesses/opportunities/threats of a borrower = SWOT analysis as part of due diligence (TYU 1).

2. Importance, timing, reasons (p.17.4–17.5)

  • Timing: thorough detailed due diligence comes after the parties have agreed in principle and reached a preliminary understanding, but before signing a binding contract.
  • Purpose: help the purchaser/investor find out all that a reasonable person can about the business before completion — critical success factors, strengths, weaknesses; expose problems to be handled in price negotiation or in warranty/indemnity clauses.
  • Four reasons: (1) confirm the business is what it appears to be; (2) identify potential "deal killer" defects and avoid a bad transaction; (3) gain information for valuing assets, defining representations and warranties, negotiating price concessions; (4) verify the transaction meets investment or acquisition criteria.
  • Due diligence vs audit (p.17.4): audit = independent examination of financial statements to express an opinion; due diligence = examination of a potential investment to confirm all material facts, reviewing financial and non-financial records as relevant.

3. Classification — seven types (p.17.5)

TypeSM keyTrigger words in the question → this one
Commercial / OperationalGenerally performed by the acquirer enterprise itself; commercial, strategic, operational view"synergies", "fit with our plants", "strategy team of the buyer"
FinancialBooks of account and financial information; performed after completion of commercial due diligence"net worth", "profit trend", "hidden liabilities"
TaxSeparate exercise but, being integral to financial status, generally included in financial due diligence; tax effect of the merger/acquisition"tax effect of merger", "regular in paying taxes"
Information SystemsAll computer systems and related matters"ERP", "systems of target"
LegalLegal aspects of functioning; property owned; compliance with statutes"title of property", "statutory compliance under various laws"
EnvironmentalEntity's environment, its flexibility and adaptiveness to the acquirer"adaptiveness to acquirer"
PersonnelPersonnel policies in line with, or changeable to suit, the restructuring"HR policies", "can policies be changed"
  • Financial due diligence ≠ overall due diligence unless its scope is wide enough; use the term with caution. Its role commences only after a price has been agreed or a restructuring plan framed. Principal objective: look behind the veil of initial information; assess benefits and costs by inquiring into past, present and future.
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Due diligence scope and reportSM Ch 17

3A. Full-fledged financial due diligence — scope (a)–(h) (p.17.7–17.13)

  • (a) History and promoters: begin here; how set up, original promoters; turning points, survival strategies, market share, product life cycle, adequacy of resources, past regulatory impact. Enquire: nature of business, locations, employment, products/markets, important suppliers, inventories, franchises/licences/patents, important expense categories, R&D, foreign currency items, legislation, information systems.
  • (b) Accounting policies: any inappropriate policy; recent changes (target may have changed them "keeping in view its intention of offering itself for sale"). Report: summary of significant policies, recent changes, areas where target's policies differ from the acquirer's, effect of differences.
  • (c) Review of FS: prepared per governing statute, Framework and AS (record deviations); operating results in great detail (price is largely based on them); extraordinary items; actual vs budget and previous period; compare trading results of past four to five years → trend of normal operating profit; benchmark with similar companies; advise indicative valuation; expert valuers for immovable property/plant; special attention to intangibles. Objective: look specifically for hidden liabilities or over-valued assets.
  • (d) Taxation: regular payment of taxes; tax effects of the merger/acquisition.
  • (e) Cash flow: internal accruals or repeated external help? Check: (a) honour commitments to trade payables, banks, government, others; (b) how well receivables and inventories are turned; (c) deployment of funds; (d) idle funds; (e) investment pattern and realisability.
  • (f) Projections: obtain for next five years with assumptions and workings, on optimistic, pessimistic and most likely bases; comment on all assumptions; unrealistic assumption → consider impact on valuation; not achievable/aggressive → say so in report; check arithmetical accuracy.
  • (g) Management and employees: excess workforce; how much labour to retain; job profiles; protracted labour litigation; PF, ESI, gratuity, leave, superannuation paid/provided/funded; unfunded gratuity → actuarial valuation from a reputed actuary; reasonableness of salary/interest/retirement assumptions; all eligible employees covered; key employees' pay, ESOPs, deferred compensation, EVA-linked pay, promised sales incentives; key employees who will not continue.
  • (h) Statutory compliance: list applicable laws + compliance checklist; irregular compliance → punitive charges, to be quantified and factored into results.
Hidden liabilities (p.17.10)Over-valued assets (p.17.11)
Show cause notices not matured into demands, not shown as contingent liabilities · Letters of comfort to banks/FIs (not "guarantees", so not disclosed) · indemnity for liabilities of subsidiaries/businesses sold · product/warranty claims, returns, liquidated damages, litigation · direct and indirect tax liabilities · long pending sales tax assessments · customs where only provisional assessment done · agreement to buy back shares at stated price · future lease liabilities · environmental/third party claims · unfunded gratuity/superannuation/leave, wrong gratuity valuation · huge labour claims after wage agreement expired · unresolved labour litigationUncollected/uncollectable receivables · obsolete/slow/non-moving inventory, inventory above NRV, huge packing material with company name · underused/obsolete P&M and spares, impaired assets · assets above market value due to capitalisation of expenditure/forex or revenue items · litigated assets · investments at cost though realisable value much lower · investments with very low return · infructuous project/deferred revenue expenditure · group company balances under reconciliation · intangible assets of no value

Examiner's twist: The case lists items and asks "which of these are hidden liabilities" — "group company balances under reconciliation" and "investments carried at cost" are in the over-valued assets list, not hidden liabilities. "Letter of comfort" is a hidden liability precisely because it is not a guarantee.

4–5. Work approach and how to conduct due diligence (p.17.13–17.15) Asked: Jan 2026 Q6(a)

  • Work approach: purchase of a business is often the largest asset purchase, so caution is needed. SM figure: Six-Dimensional Process Framework — Define, Discover, Diagnose, Design, Delivery, Defend.
  • How to conduct (write these as "steps"): start with an open mind — do not assume anything is wrong; identify trouble spots and ask for explanations · get the best team; hire due diligence experts if needed (check industry experience) · review and report on the financials submitted by the target · site visit (if applicable) · work through the process with the acquirer/investor by defining key areas · help prepare an offer after completion · get help in finance, tax, legal, marketing, technology → 360-degree view · talk to customers, suppliers, business partners, employees · risk management approach — do not antagonise the target's team with loads of questions · prepare a comprehensive report on compliances and substantive risks/issues.

6. Contents of a due diligence report (illustrative; p.17.15–17.16)

Executive summary · Introduction · Background of target · Objective of due diligence · Terms of reference and scope · Brief history · Shareholding pattern · Observations · Assessment of management structure, financial liabilities, valuation of assets · Comments on properties, leases, liens, encumbrances · Operating results · Taxation and statutory liabilities · Possible litigation liabilities · Net worth · Interlocking investments and obligations with group/associates, receivables under litigation, unprovided liabilities · SWOT · Comments on projections · Status of charges, liens, mortgages · Suggestions on affidavits, indemnities for unforeseen contingent liabilities · Suggestions before and after the merger/acquisition. Contents always vary with circumstances.

List in SMCountStarts withTrigger words → which one is asked
Reasons for due diligence4Confirm business is what it appears"why", "deal killer", "purpose"
Objectives for individual areas of verification11 bulletsBrief history of business"matters the process can include"
Scope of full-fledged financial DD(a)–(h)History of target and promoters"financial due diligence", "procedures before advising"
How to conduct~10Start with an open mind"steps", "explain to the team new to this work"

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · InvestigationSM Ch 17

7. Investigation; audit vs investigation (p.17.16–17.18)

  • Investigation = systematic, in-depth examination or inquiry to establish a fact or to evaluate a specific situation ("inquiry into facts"); an examination of books and records preliminary to financing or any other specified purpose, sometimes differing in scope from audit. Covers financing, investment decisions, fraud, profitability, cost determination.
BasisInvestigationAudit
ObjectiveEstablish a fact/happening or assess a particular situationWhether FS show true and fair view
ScopeGoverned by statute or non-statutoryWide; statutory audit scope set by law
PeriodicityNo rigid time frame; may cover several yearsQuarterly, half-yearly or yearly
NatureDetailed study of facts and figures; voluntaryTest checks/sampling; mandatory for companies
Inherent limitationsNone owing to its natureSuffers inherent limitation
EvidenceConclusivePrima facie (p.17.18: "persuasive")
Accounting principlesAnalytical; investigator not bound by accounting conventions, policies, disclosure requirementsGoverned by GAAP, audit procedures, disclosure requirements
Appointing agencyEven a third party can appointOwners/shareholders
ReportingTo person(s) on whose behalf carried outTo owners of the business
  • Mindset: investigator does not accept a stated fact until substantiated; approaches work "to suspect, verify and satisfy". Auditor relies on stated facts absent suspicious circumstances. Investigation may arise from an auditor's prima facie findings.
  • No standardised procedure, so extreme care; parties are far more likely to challenge an investigation's findings than an audit's.

8. Steps in investigation — in sequence (p.17.18–17.22)

  1. Objectives and scope: vague instructions → discuss and obtain clearly written instructions (object, scope, purpose, incidental issues); specify the period (results affected by changes since contemplated, e.g. devaluation, import restrictions, new division).
  2. Investigation programme having regard to: nature of business; structure of organisation; client's instructions; consequent scope and depth; need to extend into books of others; concentrate on relevant areas, not wide-ranging verification. Flexible; start wide and narrow down, eliminating irrelevant matters. Ghost-worker example: time and job cards, appointment/termination, attendance, internal check, wage sheets, bank withdrawals, actual disbursement.
  3. Collection of evidence: oral explanations from personnel; if client is external, get the matter formally agreed by the business through the client; most convincing evidence; all available evidence; elimination and corroboration; not restricted to prima facie evidence; may obtain specially procured evidence; external experts (related parties outside, valuers); must not interfere with client's regular work. Land valuation: records, bids received, neighbourhood prices — even land registration office; expert land valuer.
  4. Analysis and interpretation: well reasoned, backed by established facts and records; no pre-conceived notions; consider political/economic factors, competition, historical pattern, nature of business; brief, clear, free from doubt.
  5. Report: addressed to the party at whose instance done; governed by (a) client's instructions, (b) findings. Rules: nothing irrelevant · words chosen to avoid other meanings · facts and conclusions linked with evidence · bases and assumptions explicit, not in conflict with the objective (over-stocking case: do not assume bin-card ordering levels are a fair guide); assumptions only when unavoidable · nature, objective, scope, limitations · paragraph form with headings; detail in annexures · restrictions imposed by client, with reasons and impact · opinion in the final paragraph.

9. Special issues in investigation (p.17.22–17.24)

IssueSM answer — the line that scores
100% or selective verificationDepends on case: cashier's defalcation → examine all cash vouchers; profitability → selective, but nothing material concealed. Prefer statistically recognised sampling over so-called "test checks".
Reliance on audited accountsLaunched because of doubt in audited accounts → no reliance. Valuation of business/share/goodwill for incoming partner → ordinarily may rely unless audit looks casual or terms require fresh testing. Cases: Short & Compton v. Brackert (1904); Mead v. Ball Baker & Co. (1911) — old, so get client's instruction in writing in advance. Unaudited → duty to check. Specially prepared by a professional accountant knowing the purpose → may accept (Hedley Byrne), after checking objectivity/bias.
ExpertsWritten general consent of client at the beginning; settle costs.
Disputes, conflicting claimsStay above disputes; seller wants high price, buyer wants value depressed; keep interest of all parties in view.
Basis of opinionNo speculative opinion; only established facts; if facts cannot be established → no opinion, or qualify with reasons (incomplete records).
Futuristic statementsRefuse even if appointing authority wants it; may assume established trend continues in the near future (absent contrary evidence) for present value; must not project the trend into future years.
Working papersRetain full notes, schedules, record of conversations; link book figures to final figures; representation letter from appointing authority; needed when giving evidence in court (e.g. value of shares of a company taken over by Government).
AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Business investigation, typesSM Ch 17

10. Business investigation — items and numbers (p.17.24–17.32) Asked: May 2026 Q6(b)

  • Overall picture first (figures are only symbols): group chart of companies and inter-company terms; product lines; imported vs local inputs; labour intensity and union relations; distribution method; character of management; economic and political forces; market position. Check capital adequacy, sales trend, return on capital, whether at 100% capacity.
  • P&L summary for say 5 to 7 years; inventory consumed per product instead of opening/closing; consistent inventory valuation; GP and expense-to-sales ratios. Turnover product-wise; few large vs many small customers; Order Books for fictitious sales; split dissimilar activities. Wage structure (industrial award), labour disputes. Depreciation consistent and adequate; revalued assets → depreciation on increased value adjusted; maintenance should rise with age; dilapidation provision for leasehold. Managerial remuneration excessive or nil → adjust. Exceptional/non-recurring items adjusted; check IT assessment orders for disallowed items. Major repairs every 3 or 4 years. Fluctuating results → may take 9 to 10 years; eliminate years vitiated by long industrial dispute, natural calamity, pandemic, fire, war.
  • Fixed assets (May 2026): obtain age analysis; old/obsolete need replacement; value not above service value for balance active life plus scrap value; title deeds (e.g. jointly owned land and building); maintenance history → allowance for heavy repairs; revaluation → increased depreciation adjusted against profit; recoverable amount below carrying amount → impairment per AS 28. Plus idle assets: plant used long after acquisition, assets awaiting installation, surplus cash, slow inventory — eliminate from net worth, add proper value separately.
  • Investments: current = readily realisable, held ≤ one year, lower of cost and fair value (individual or category, not overall); long-term at cost less permanent decline; dividend out of pre-acquisition profits reduces cost. Inventories: consistent, no profit element, allowance for damaged/slow stock, physical check where held by others, WIP. Receivables: bad-debt provision in year of sale; lengthening credit/discounts = falling demand; ageing; debtors-to-sales ratio. Money in a bank in liquidation only to extent of Deposit Insurance. Liabilities: suppression or free reserves; long overdue. Taxation: reopened assessments; disregard temporary tax benefit. Capital: well balanced (not top-heavy with debentures/preference capital); transfer restriction depresses value.
  • Future turnover factors: Trend, Marketability, Political and economic considerations, Competition. Working capital: inventory/turnover ratio trend; creditors backlog; effect of more turnover/new products. Future maintainable profit: falling profit-to-capital % with causes continuing → further capital not commercially advisable.

11. Types of investigation (p.17.32–17.58)

  • Statutory: inspector by Central Government order (s.210); SFIO (s.212); other cases (s.213); ownership of a company (s.216) — detail in Self-Paced Module Set A. Non-statutory (6): incoming partner; share valuation of private companies; bank/FI proposing loan; frauds; buying a business; review of profit/financial forecast.
  • 11.1 Incoming partner: terms reasonable? continuing return and safety of capital. Steps include: history of firm; partnership deed (capital, drawings, retirement benefits, goodwill); profitability with partners' remuneration adjusted; tangible asset backing (less important for service firms); orders and clientele; loan finance; key personnel; contractual obligations to employees; reasons for offer of admission (does it coincide with a senior partner's retirement?); capital employed and return vs alternatives; specialisation; goodwill computation on admission/retirement; concessional-entry clauses; incomplete contracts. Personal considerations count predominantly.
  • 11.2 Shares of private companies: transfer restricted → no free market. Net worth basis (assets revalued at replacement cost less notional depreciation; goodwill = super-profit capacity) or yield basis (average profit of 5 to 7 years capitalised; higher rate → lower value; risk and transfer restriction reflected in the rate; rate from quoted similar companies). Control passing → value control separately and add. Preference shares: yield on similar preference shares.
  • 11.3 Bank/FI loan: purpose and use; repayment schedule and its assumptions; standing of directors; MoA/AoA authority to borrow; history and performance of past 5 years; effect of economic/political/social changes during loan period; earlier rejection by another lender and reasons. Steps: condensed income statement for previous five years (maintainable profit increased by benefit of borrowed funds); ratios — sales/average inventory, sales/fixed assets, equity/fixed assets, current ratio, quick ratio, equity/long-term loans, sales/book debts, ROCE; product-wise sales. Schedules: fixed assets (encumbrance, revaluation date), inventory (slow items, pledges), receivables split — credit period not expired / due within six months / over six months, dues from directors; investments; secured/unsecured loans (unsecured loan repayable before bank loan → verify terms); tax; liabilities with ageing of payables; insurance adequacy; undisclosed contingent liabilities.
  • 11.5 Buying a business: same lines as share valuation, plus (A) proprietary/partnership: reasons for sale and effect of proprietor's retirement; lease length and renewal; unexpired patents; age of managerial staff and unprovided pension/gratuity; dependence on a few customers; goodwill. (B) Limited company: authorised and issued capital; uncalled liability; class rights; dividends and arrears on cumulative preference; search of Register of Charges at ROC; offer price (unquoted shares need valuation).
  • 11.6 Profit forecasts: not verifiable like financial statements of a completed period; special review, departs from expressing an opinion on past events.

Examiner's twist: A 5-mark case about a client who wants the investigator to "project the next five years of profit to fix the price" is really testing special issue (f): refuse to be futuristic. A case where the client's auditors are under suspicion is testing issue (b): no reliance on those audited accounts.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Fraud investigationSM Ch 17

11.4 Investigation of frauds (p.17.41–17.56) Asked: Sep 2025 Q6(c) OR; Nov 2024 3(c)

  • Fraud in the Companies Act: s.143(10) (SAs deemed auditing standards) and s.447 (punishment). SA 240 para 11(a): "an intentional act by one or more individuals among management, TCWG, employees, or third parties, involving the use of deception to obtain an unjust or illegal advantage." s.447: act, omission, concealment of fact or abuse of position, with intent to deceive, gain undue advantage, or injure interests of company/shareholders/creditors/others — "whether or not there is any wrongful gain or wrongful loss".
  • Broad types: fraudulent financial reporting; misappropriation of assets; corruption (conflicts of interest, bribery, illegal gratuities, economic extortion). Personal-gain fraud: bribery (official bribery → company usually criminally prosecuted). Corporate: advance billing, shell/dummy companies, money laundering (PMLA 2002). Operational level: tampering of cheques/online payments, off-book frauds (cash taken before recording; prove by circumstantial evidence, customer confirmations, customer copies, background checks), cash misappropriation (after recording; surprise checks), teeming and lading, fraudulent disbursements, expense reimbursement, payroll, commission schemes (salesman inflates sales by fictitious billing, alters prices, shares volumes; need money trails across entities and geographies).
  • Procedure before investigating: ascertain the exact duties of the suspect, his relation to office routine, circumstances in which defalcation came to light; greater the authority, wider the field. Nature unknown → study financial and accounting structure, line of responsibility, internal control weaknesses; direct enquiry where there is excessive control in a single person without supervision.
AreaHow fraud is done (SM)Verification (SM)
Cash receiptsFull receipt, part on counterfoil; larger discount; fictitious goods-returned credit; cash sale shown as credit sale; good debt written off; short-debiting customer; under-casting receipts/over-casting payments; short carry-forward of page totalsInventory, sales summaries, rental registers, salesmen's advices, counterfoils; "duplicate" carbon copies; recall paying-in slips and compare with Cash Book; scrap sales, rent from labourers, refunds of deposits; vouch cash sales; check withdrawals with pass book
Cash paymentsDouble/false invoice payment; personal expenses (betting losses as advertisement); withdrawing unclaimed customer credits; false refund; wrong wage-sheet totalsParty acknowledgements, confirm altered receipts with party; bearer cheques; wage system (over-totalling, dummy workmen); goods ordered vs received; confirmations from partners/directors; petty cash; salary paid only to persons in service
Suppliers' ledgerFictitious/duplicate invoices; suppressing suppliers' credit notes; withdrawing unclaimed amounts; inflated prices with cash kickbackVouch Purchase Journal with Goods Inward Book and invoices; statements of account from all suppliers; allowances authorised; internal control on purchase orders, collusion with suppliers
Customers' ledgerTeeming and lading; allowance/rebate credit to cover collection; crediting another customer then withdrawingAllowances, goods returned, bad debts written off; Order Book vs Sales Day Book; confirmations — including customers with nil balance (send them statements)
InventoryRemoval; written off as damaged; records manipulated to tally; inflated issues to production; dispatched but not billedReview receipt-storage-dispatch system; first establish items and quantities missing (physical vs Inventory Book); duties of stock handlers; Goods Inward/Outward Registers; returns; reconcile shortages; management diversion via inflated wastage → engineer's assistance, past wastage, machine hour capacity
  • Indicators: discrepancies in records · conflicting or missing evidence · unacceptable management responses (denial of access, undue time pressure, delays, denial of CAATs, unwillingness to fix control deficiencies) · others (policies at variance with industry, frequent changes in estimates).
  • Fraud Diamond (Wolfe and Hermanson): Incentive, Opportunity, Rationalization, Capability — capability is the fourth element added.
  • Ability to detect depends on: skill of perpetrator, frequency and extent of manipulation, degree of collusion, relative size of amounts, seniority of those involved. Detection risk can only be reduced, not eliminated.

Examiner's twist: "Suggest one procedure" for rising manpower cost in an ETP section with punched timecards (TYU 2): analyse in/out times and make a surprise visit to count actual workers → ghost workers. Story about "salesmen's commission" (Sep 2025) → commission scheme; write fictitious billing, altered prices, shared volumes, then the procedure (duties of suspect, single-person control) and verification of customers' ledger and confirmations.

AAA LDR — Ss
AAA · Twist notes · Forensic accounting and FAISSM Ch 17

12–13. Forensic accounting and the forensic professional (p.17.58–17.66) Asked: May 2026 MCQ CS-III Q12

  • "Forensic" = suitable for use in a court of law. Bologna: application of financial skills and an investigative mentality to unresolved issues, within the context of the rules of evidence. Hopwood, Leiner and Young: application of investigative and analytical skills to resolve financial issues in a manner that meets standards required by courts.
  • Red flags: warnings, not proof of fraud; categories — financial performance, accounting system, operational, behavioural.
  • Why audits miss fraud: designed to detect material misstatements, not immaterial frauds; sampling; reliance on audit trail rather than events behind documents.
  • Forensic professional: first consider whether the firm has the skills and experience to accept; may be in practice or employed by insurers, banks, police, government. Services: FS manipulations, fund diversion/asset tracing, anti-money laundering, licence fees/dues/tax evasion, related party transactions/valuations, loss/damage estimation, suspicious transactions under IBC. Involved in: expert testimony, fraud prevention, computer forensics, fraud detection. Must see substance over form.
  • PLI insurance claim (TYU 3): third parties unwilling to give documents; independence paramount; insured may negotiate to save reputation; quantification per policy terms; date of first claim under the "claim series" clause.
PointStatutory auditForensic accounting
Mandate / whoLegal mandate; only by a CA; independent opinion on FS; ICAI auditing standardsTo support legal cases; non-CAs permitted; report findings of evidence; primarily financials; ICAI standards (FAIS)
Objective / focusTrue and fair; very general overall reviewDiscover facts and evidence; narrow — validate transactions and balances
Approach / targetControl and substantive tests; material misstatementsFocused testing to confirm suspicion/allegation; identify/confirm nature of violation
Skills / presumptionTesting, analysis, inquiry, observation; professional scepticism, due careScrutiny, fact-finding, interviews; neutrality; "investigative mentality"
OutcomeAudit report opinion (qualified "except for")Present evidence to a court — "they shall judge"; no opinion
Stock, estimatesRelies on management representationIndependent verification of suspected items
Off-balance sheet itemsVouch arithmetic accuracy and compliance with proceduresRegularity and propriety of the transactions examined
Adverse findingsNegative or qualified opinionLegal determination of fraud impact and identification of perpetrators (per scope)
Period / techniqueAn accounting period; sample basedNo such limit; in-depth checking; seemingly immaterial items too

14. Process of forensic accounting — 6 steps (p.17.66–17.68)

  1. Initialization: clarify motive, purpose, utility; meet client; conflict check as soon as parties known; preliminary investigation before detailed plan.
  2. Develop plan: objectives and methodology.
  3. Obtain relevant evidence: enough to prove identity of fraudster(s), mechanics of scheme, amount of loss; usable in court within time; clear chain of custody — gaps → evidence challenged or inadmissible; watch for falsified/destroyed documents.
  4. Perform analysis: economic damages, summarising transactions, asset tracing, present value with discount rates, regression/sensitivity, computer models, charts.
  5. Reporting: findings, summary of evidence, amount of loss, those involved; scope, approach, limitations; how scheme set up and controls circumvented; recommend control improvements.
  6. Court proceedings: team may be called to describe evidence and how the suspect was identified.

15. FAIS 510 — Reporting Results (p.17.68–17.70)

  • Written report, clear and accurate, findings on reliable and relevant evidence, precise and unambiguous. One engagement with several assignments → multiple reports. Addressed to Primary Stakeholders; shared with others if required/permissible. No fixed format; if stakeholders or law mandate a format, follow it keeping key elements.
  • Key elements: title, addressee, distribution list · scope and objectives · approach and broad procedures · executive summary · reference to expert used · statement of conduct per FAIS or material departures · findings with key evidence and sources · assumptions, limitations, disclaimers · conclusions (if any).
  • Draft discussion: where mandate requires discussion with the subject party, include a summary of responses; principles of natural justice require discussion (may be done by stakeholders via disciplinary committee/show-cause notice).
  • Limitations: lack of management support, denied access, court orders, short timelines → disclaimers in report. Report shall not express an opinion or pass judgement on guilt or innocence; culpability is for internal disciplinary or judicial process.
  • Issue within reasonable time per engagement terms; interim reports allowed without compromising the investigation and are subject to this Standard.

16. FAIS framework and series (p.17.70–17.73)

  • Framework: Code of Ethics is the foundation; four pillars — (i) Basic Principles, (ii) Key Concepts, (iii) Standards, (iv) Guidance. All mandatory except Guidance (recommendatory). FAIS provide: minimum standards to professionals; quality indication to users; appreciation to regulators/government; implementation guidance to everyone. Principle-based, not rule-based. Unable to comply/conflict with law → FAI report draws attention to material departures with explanation.
  • Series: 100 Key Concepts (110 Nature of Engagement, 120 Fraud Risk, 130 Laws and Regulations, 140 Applying Hypotheses) · 200 Engagement Management (210 Objectives, 220 Acceptance and Appointment, 230 Using Work of an Expert, 240 Engaging with Agencies, 250 Communication with Stakeholders) · 300 Executing (310 Planning, 320 Evidence and Documentation, 330 Work Procedures, 340 Interviews, 350 Review and Supervision, 360 Testifying before a Competent Authority) · 400 Specialised (410 Data Analysis, 420 Digital Domain, 430 Loans or Borrowings) · 500 Reporting (510) · 600 Quality Control (610).

Examiner's twist: "Management is confused whether to get an audit or forensic accounting done for suspicious building-material purchases" (TYK 17) — answer is the forensic vs audit difference, not investigation steps. "Team did not record who collected ERP logs and when" (TYK 18) — the one line: chain of custody broken → evidence may be challenged or become inadmissible, defeating the engagement.

Beyond the SM (practical): engagement letters for forensic work usually fix the evidence-handling log format at the start, so custody gaps are avoided.

AAA · Twist notes · Audit of PSUsSM Ch 15

15Ch 15 — Overview of Audit of Public Sector Undertakings

Asked: Sep 2025 Q2(c)(i)–(ii) compliance audit concept, perspective, regularity and propriety (2+2) · Jan 2026 Q3(a) propriety audit and its principles (5m) · May 2026 MCQ Q15 committee for policies not giving desired results (Estimates Committee) · May 2026 Q6(c) OR performance audit, efficiency aspects (4m).

1–2. PSUs and the framework (p.15.4–15.6)

  • PSUs: owned/controlled by Central Government, State Government(s), or partly Central and partly State. Three categories: (1) departmentally managed undertakings (Indian Railways, Postal Services, Security Printing Press, Canteen Stores Department); (2) government companies and deemed government companies under the Companies Act, 2013; (3) statutory corporations under specific Acts (LIC, UTI).
  • Audit by C&AG through the Indian Audit and Accounts Department. C&AG (DPC) Act, 1971: term six years or up to age 65, whichever is earlier; may resign by letter addressed to the President.
  • Audited organisations: all Union/State departments (incl. Railways, Posts and Telecom); government companies and corporations; non-commercial autonomous bodies owned/controlled by Union/States; authorities substantially financed from Union/State revenues.
ArticleContentTrigger → this Article
148Appointment by President; removal only for proven misbehaviour or incapacity (special procedure); salary/conditions by Parliament"appointment", "removal"
149Duties and powers re accounts of Union, States, other bodies as prescribed by Parliament's law (DPC Act, 1971)"duties and powers"
150President prescribes form of accounts on C&AG's advice"form of accounts"
151Reports on Union/State accounts submitted to President/Governor, who causes them to be laid before Parliament/Legislature"to whom will the report be submitted"

Audit of government companies (commercial audit) (p.15.6; TYU 2, TYK 5 answers)

  • Government equity 51% or more: auditors are CA firms appointed by the C&AG, who (i) gives them directions on the manner of audit, (ii) may comment upon their audit reports, (iii) has a right to conduct a supplementary audit and (iv) to cause test audit if considered necessary, by an order.
  • s.2(45) (TYK 5 answer p.15.36): not less than 51% of paid-up share capital held by Central Government, or any State Government(s), or partly by Central and partly by State Government(s); includes a subsidiary of such a government company. Listing on a stock exchange is irrelevant — a 60:40 disinvested listed company stays under C&AG.
  • s.143(5) (TYU 2 answer p.15.36): in a government company, C&AG directs the manner of audit; auditor's report includes the directions, action taken thereon and its impact on the accounts and financial statements. SM examples of directions: transactions processed outside the IT system; loan restructuring/waiver/write-off due to inability to repay; grants for specific schemes accounted/used as per terms.
  • Opening story (p.15.3): a State report includes impact of significant comments arising from supplementary audit — e.g. improper depreciation leading to profit overstated by ₹20 crore; a test audit of an insurance PSU checked compliance with regulatory norms (compliance audit in nature).

Not in the SM text for this chapter: the SM Ch 15 text does not set out ss.139(5), 139(7), 143(6) or 143(7) (appointment time limits, comment/supplementary audit time limits, test audit order). It mentions supplementary and test audit only as above, and s.143(5) only in the TYU 2 answer. Do not write subsection details from these notes; check the Companies Act chapter of your SM before relying on them.

SM wordingC&AG "has a right to conduct a supplementary audit of such companies"; reviews accounts of selected government companies along with the report of the statutory auditors (Key Takeaways)C&AG may "cause test audit if considered necessary, by an order"
